# Runtime contract

## What the runtime is

The runtime runs work without a person at the keyboard and keeps the person in charge of what matters. A person writes a request; code plans it as tasks from a flow file; each task runs as one skill, once, in the same container and through the same entry in which the skill was proven in the lab; what the run leaves comes back to the project by one rule of paths; and the task then waits for the person on a pending decision: a question to answer, or a delivery to release or send back. Releasing is not approving: a released delivery stays a draft in the project's state file. The runtime names no AI tool: the model and its adapter come from the gate file, `evals/eval-gate.json`, and adapters do the rest.

Two runtimes exist today. The first runtime (`scripts/runtime.py`) runs one agent on social comments and stays as it is until that agent moves to the container (stage 7 of the platform plan, `docs/architecture/platform-plan-2026-10-05.md` (removed on 2026-10-15; `git show bc830832:docs/architecture/platform-plan-2026-10-05.md`)). The task runtime (`runtime/`) began as stage 1 of that plan, an end-to-end skeleton accepted on 2026-10-05 (`docs/architecture/skeleton-findings-2026-10-05.md` (removed on 2026-10-14; `git show d3e60770:docs/architecture/skeleton-findings-2026-10-05.md`)), and holds the stages built since ("Pieces" below). Both keep their records in one store, `providers/store/sqlite.py`.

## The first runtime (until stage 7)

The runtime starts agents without a person at the keyboard: a scheduler fires, the runtime finds new work, runs an agent on it through an adapter, and executes the agent's proposal only when a deterministic gate allows it. It names no AI tool; adapters do. Decided on 2026-09-28 (`docs/decisions.md`, "An agent runtime as a new, tool-free layer"); first built for one agent, `social-manager` (backlog PB7 (removed on 2026-10-15; `git show bc830832:docs/backlog.md`; folded into PB6)), on a Mac with the launchd scheduler.

### Pieces

| Piece | Where | What it does |
|-------|-------|--------------|
| Trigger | `scheduler:job` class, a recurring job | runs `scripts/runtime.py tick` every N minutes |
| Runtime | `scripts/runtime.py` | finds new work, records it, runs the agent, applies the gate, executes, logs |
| Store | `store:runtime` class (`providers/store/`) | events, cursors, runs, inbox, actions; many writers at once |
| Agent run | `runtime/cli.py contained-run` (the first runtime, `scripts/runtime.py`, until WP-7.5: `adapters/<harness>/run-agent.sh`) | runs one skill on one task in the eval container, on a copy of the artifacts it declares, and returns its answer |
| Gate | the skill's own script (`mkt-engage/scripts/policy_gate.py`) | decides whether a proposal is inside the person's approval |
| Actuator | a provider (`publisher:<platform>`) | executes, with an idempotency key |
| Configuration | `docs/workbench/runtime.json` in the project | paths, model, budgets, and optionally the implementation of a class; no secret. Its keys `mailbox`, `store`, `scheduler` and `publisher` are configuration keys, not class names, and the file's keys are closed (an unknown key is refused, and the error names the nearest known one): the runtime maps them to `reader:email`, `store:runtime`, `scheduler:job` and `publisher:<platform>` |
| Provider resolution | `providers/resolve.py` | turns a requirement class into the provider script; the runtime builds no provider path itself |

### An agent run

- **Input:** the agent definition (`agents/<name>.md`), a task file the runtime writes (what to do, and the trigger's data marked as external content), the skills the agent lists, and the project folder, which the runtime passes to the adapter as `--project`. The runtime itself confines nothing: what the model can read, inside the project and outside it, is what the adapter and its harness allow (each adapter's README says how far that goes; containing a runtime agent beyond its tool list is backlog N15 (removed on 2026-10-15; `git show bc830832:docs/backlog.md`), which "The contained run" below closes). The social handler (`runtime/handlers/social.py`, `social_vote.py`) runs its agent as that operation: `contained-run` with the skill `mkt-engage` or `mkt-vote-round` and the task text of the measured cases, so what this section says about the adapter holds only for the first runtime (`scripts/runtime.py`), which stays as the rollback until WP-7.5. A contained run takes no agent file and no model from the configuration: the skill must be in the pack of an enabled agent of the `area_agents` section of `runtime.json`, and the model comes from the skill's proof.
- **Tools:** in the first runtime's adapter run, read only: the model reads files and answers, and cannot write files, run commands or call a network service. In a contained run the model can read, answer and run commands inside the container, on the copy, with no credential and not the open network; nothing but its reply comes back (the four restrictions of "The contained run"). Either way everything it proposes comes back as a fenced JSON block in its answer, whose shape the task names.
- **Budget:** a spend limit per run (`max_cost_usd`; a contained run has none: its bound is one attempt within `timeout_seconds`, and a run on a subscription or free credential has no billed cost) and a time limit (`timeout_seconds`); a daily spend cap across runs (`daily_cost_cap_usd`), checked before each run from the store. A run whose cost is unknown (the adapter has no price for the model, the run timed out, or it never ended) counts as `max_cost_usd_per_run`, and the tick's output says how many such runs the day has (`runs_without_cost_today`).
- **Output:** `<out>/response.md`, `<out>/timing.json` (`total_tokens`, `duration_ms`, `cost_usd`, `exit_code`), `<out>/raw.json` and `<out>/stderr.log`. The runtime keeps `<out>` under the run's folder and records the run in the store.

Adapter entry point (the first runtime's; the handler's agent run is `contained-run`, below):

```text
run-agent.sh --agent-file <agents/name.md> --task-file <f> --project <dir> --model <id> --out <dir>
             [--skill-dir <dir>]... [--max-cost-usd <amount>] [--timeout-seconds <n>]
```

It copies the skills (never links them) into a fresh working folder for the run, names `--project` to the harness as a folder to read, allows only reading tools (how far they reach outside `--project` is the harness's doing, and the adapter's README says it), loads no connectors and no user-level settings, and writes the files above. It exits 0 when the model answered, 1 otherwise.

### Why the model never acts

The trigger's data is written by strangers (comments, e-mails). A model that could run the publisher could be talked into publishing. So the model proposes; code decides and executes:

1. The model returns a proposal (a category, a language, a reply text).
2. The runtime writes the proposal's text to a file and runs the gate script with the approved policy. The policy file is bound by hash to what the person approved (its `policy:<sha256>` in the state file's standing approval); the gate script is bound only for a scheduled tick that carries `--pin` (below).
3. Only a gate result of `auto` makes the runtime call the actuator, with the file the gate checked and an idempotency key. Anything else goes to the inbox with the gate's reasons.

### What the approval of a recurring tick covers

The person approves the tick once, when it is scheduled (`scheduler:job`, `--every`), and it then runs unattended with the publishing credential. That approval covers:

- what the scheduler hashes: the command's arguments, the program, the scheduler's runner, and the files in the command file's snapshot (`runtime.py`, `runtime_vote.py`, `redact.py`, `providers/resolve.py`, the pin file). The job runs its own copies of them, and a firing whose hashes differ is refused;
- with `--pin <file>`: `docs/workbench/runtime.json` and the gate script (`mkt-engage/scripts/policy_gate.py` of the workbench `runtime.json` names), by the sha256 that `runtime.py pin` recorded in the pin file. Before anything else, every firing hashes both again and refuses (exit 3, nothing runs) when either differs. A tick without `--pin`, run by hand, checks nothing.

It does not cover the rest of what a firing loads from the workbench checkout that `runtime.json` names: the notification parser, the platform's data file (`shared/references/platforms/<publisher>.json`, from which the tick reads the platform's limits and which it passes to the skills' scripts), the adapter's `run-agent.sh` (the first runtime's agent run), for the handler's contained run `runtime/cli.py` with the modules it loads, the skill that runs and the platform references staged with it, and the eval image, and the agent file and its skills, the providers (the publisher included), the vote step's scripts, and the folders of `path`. A vote job, once approved, runs its own verified copy of the data file. A change to them (a pull in that checkout) takes effect at the next firing, without a new approval. So does a change of the model of a contained run: it comes from the skill's proof, not from the configuration, which the pin covers. Keep that checkout on a reviewed revision; to bind one of those files, add it to the pin first.

A change to `runtime.json` or to the gate script stops the tick until the person reviews it, runs `runtime.py pin` again and schedules the tick again with the new pin file: that is the new approval.

### Records

- Every trigger item is an event (deduplicated by source and external id), claimed atomically, and ends `done`, `failed` or `to_inbox`.
- Every run: agent, event, start and end, status, exit code, cost, tokens, output folder.
- Every outward action: kind, idempotency key, target, payload hash, provider result.
- Every item for the person: the inbox, with the payload and its hash; the person approves or rejects it, and an approved item runs only if its hash still matches.

### The weekly vote step

When `runtime.json` has a `vote` section (`repo`, `branch`, `pillars`, optional `pillar_aliases`, `image`, `card_html`), each tick also runs the weekly vote step (`scripts/runtime_vote.py`; design in `docs/architecture/weekly-vote.md`):

1. Code reads the vote files from the repository (the `integration:vcs` provider's `read-file`, read only) and runs `mkt-vote-round`'s `vote_state.py`. Nothing pending, or a round already handled (cursor `vote:<round>`), ends the step. The cursor is written only once the round has an inbox item, so a failure before that leaves the round for the next tick; `reject` on a vote item clears it (the store's `cursor-clear`), and the next tick redoes the round.
2. The agent runs read-only with `mkt-vote-round` and returns one `vote-proposal` block. The runtime checks it against the state (the winner's text or one of the three options with a reason, the slot's language, the next pillar, three non-empty options) and never repairs it.
3. Code builds everything the approval covers: the content file, `check_post.py`, the post image (`render.py`; without a browser the post goes text-only and the item says so), the next round's queue file (`vote_update.py --queue-round`, which refuses used topics), the publish job (which carries the publisher's idempotency ledger, as the publisher's dry run names it, so that the job at its slot uses the ledger it was approved with), and one bundle file. The bundle's sha256 is the inbox item's hash. A failed check leaves the item not ready: it cannot be approved.
4. `approve --id <n> --confirmed --sha256 <hash>` on a `vote` item re-hashes every file, refuses when the queue file in the repository moved, schedules the job at the slot (the scheduler's dry run, then the confirmed call with its digest) and commits the queue file (`commit-files`, only `data/pick-queue.json`).
5. At the slot, `scripts/vote_job.py`, scheduled with the system interpreter's fixed path and carrying the folders of `runtime.json`'s `path` (the scheduler's own `PATH` is short), publishes the post (first comment, image), reads the vote files again and commits only `post_url` on the round, the post in `posts.json` and its image. The post counts as published as soon as the publisher prints its address: when only the first comment failed, the job still records the post, then exits 1 and reports the comment's error. A failure after publishing prints what to record by hand.

### Safety rules

- The runtime never publishes without a gate result of `auto` under an active approval, or an inbox item the person approved with a matching hash.
- The runtime never publishes a text in which the shared credential formats (`scripts/redact.py`) match: the model that drafted it can read files, and a comment can ask it to quote one. A reply with such a match is not sent, by the tick or by `approve`; it goes to the inbox with the value masked and no reply file, and the person answers by hand. A vote proposal with one is masked in everything built from it, and its item cannot be approved.
- The runtime never runs a model on the person's own machine (`local/<name>@<hash>`, evals/local_models.py): the lab facade refuses it with "a local model is for lab tests only; the runtime does not run one", and `auto` ranks only models with a price in `runtime/prices.json`, which a local model never has.
- A proposal block that is missing, malformed or has fields outside the task's shape sends the event to the inbox; the runtime never repairs a proposal.
- A mailbox that cannot be read (an expired authorization, the network) does not stop the tick: pasted comments and the vote step still run, the mailbox cursor stays where it was, the tick's output carries `mailbox: {status: failed, note}`, and the person is notified at most once a day.
- The mailbox cursor never moves past a message that was not read. The mailbox answers newest first and says when older messages were left out; the tick reads on until none is, and when it cannot (a later search fails, or more wait than a tick reads) it keeps what it read as events, leaves the cursor where it was and says `mailbox: {status: incomplete, note}`.
- An error the runtime did not expect, in one event or in the vote step, does not end the tick: the event ends `failed` with the error's type, a run that was open ends `failed`, the traceback goes to stderr, and the next event is handled.
- The daily cost cap stops new runs; pending events wait for the next day or for the person.
- Configuration holds paths and limits only; credentials come through `providers/secrets/resolver.py`.
- One tick at a time per project (a lock); the scheduler also refuses overlapping firings.

## Pieces

The modules of the task runtime that exist, and what each owns. A later stage adds modules; each is listed here when it is built.

| Path | What it owns |
|---|---|
| `runtime/lab.py` | The lab facade: the only file of `runtime/` that reads anything under `evals/`. Runs one skill once in the eval container; the pause on the account limit, the refusals, the early end and the stopping are the lab's own functions. Never builds or pulls the eval image: a missing one is an error that names the setup command (`python3 scripts/image_lock.py pull` for the published image the lock file names, `python3 evals/executor.py ensure` otherwise) |
| `runtime/ops.py` | The operations layer: every operation a shell can perform (request, route, approve, reject, run the next task, pending, answer, release, retry, cancel, status, the configuration, the mirrors, the file drop, the dependencies, the standing approvals, the proof, the verdict, progress, the dispatcher's jobs, the conversation, the contained run, and what the local service reads: `config`, `task`, `flows`, `stop-runs`, and the reads of the views: `agents`, `conversation`, `skills`, `costs`, `connections`, `artifacts`, `artifact`, and `version`, the change signal; a pending decision carries `actions`). Every shell calls it; no shell reaches the store, the facade or a project's files by itself. It is `ops.py` and its siblings (the `ops_*.py` files, each listed in `runtime/README.md`; the first are these): the conversation is `runtime/ops_say.py`, the reads of the views and the local service are `runtime/ops_reads.py`, the derived waits are `runtime/ops_waits.py`, the configuration's acceptance is `runtime/ops_config.py`, the runs of tasks and the dispatcher's round are `runtime/ops_runs.py`, cancelling a request and stopping a task at any moment are `runtime/ops_cancel.py` (with `runtime/run_stop.py`, the signals and the router's watcher), and the names every part shares, the locks among them, are `runtime/ops_core.py`; `ops.py` exposes them under their names, so a shell imports `ops` only |
| `runtime/ops_core.py` | The names the operations layer shares, once (the checkout, `OpsError`, the project's context, the store call, the two locks, the runtime's own key, the data-folder names). Every module reads them as `core.<name>` at call time and never binds one at import, so that a patch of `ops_core.<name>` reaches every reader; it holds no operation and imports no other file of the layer |
| `runtime/ops_say.py` | The conversation with the planning agent (`say`, `chat_memory`), its queue while a run holds the project (`route_queued`) and the answers written by code from the store's records (a question about the state, a direct turn), a part of the operations layer that `ops.py` exposes; it reaches `ops.py` and `ops_turn.py` at call time only; the order in which a line is taken is the stage `ops` of `turn_order.py` |
| `runtime/ops_waits.py` | The derived waits in the operations layer ("Derived waits" below): `rederive`, `for_plan`, `for_task`, `annotate` (a plan decision with its waits), `approve_plan` (the tasks created with their waits and the go-ahead), `go_ahead` (a created task), `task_row`, `waiting_for` (the read); a part of the operations layer that `ops.py` hooks into, it reaches no operation of `ops.py` |
| `runtime/ops_config.py` | The configuration's acceptance in the operations layer ("The configuration, and who may accept a change of it" below): `accept_config`, `set_mode` (with `accept` for the person in the terminal) and `_record_acceptance`, the one writer of the accepted hash and of who accepted it, and `write_checkpoints`; a part of the operations layer that `ops.py` exposes, it reaches no operation of `ops.py` |
| `runtime/ops_runs.py` | The runs of tasks and the dispatcher's round, a part of the operations layer that `ops.py` exposes (`run_next`, `run_task`, `dispatch`, `reap`): one task run in its own lock (`task-<id>.lock`), admission and the claim under the claim lock, the recovery of what dead processes left, the children the round starts (one OS process per task run, `cli.py run-task`), their reaping, the report of the runs that ended, and the stop of the children; it reaches `ops.py` at call time only |
| `runtime/parallel.py` | The rules of tasks that run at once, pure: a skill's footprint (the files its tasks may write), whether two declared paths can name the same file, and `admit`: a ready task starts only when the project has a free slot (`max_running`), its agent has a free slot (`max_parallel`), no code task of its request runs and no running task's footprint overlaps its own |
| `runtime/ops_reads.py` | The reads of the views and the local service (`version`, `stop_runs`, `commands`, `agents`, `conversation`, `skills`, `costs`, `connections`, `service_check`, `artifacts`, `artifact`, `artifact_raw`), a part of the operations layer that `ops.py` exposes; it reaches `ops.py` at call time only |
| `runtime/operations.py` | The table of operations (a pure literal): one row per operation of `ops.py` with its verb, arguments, the channels that may call it (`terminal`, `chat`, `page`, `mcp`, `messaging`; an effect is approved with its hash, in the terminal or on the local page, or on a messaging app that the project grants that effect's word), whether it calls a model and, as `job`, whether the local service runs it as a job. Every shell derives what it accepts from it, and the texts that name a command are built from it |
| `runtime/cli.py` | The terminal shell: its parser is built from the table, one command per operation, one JSON object printed |
| `runtime/service.py` | The local service: the operations layer through an API on `127.0.0.1` (and, only with the opt-in `--lan`, one private LAN address), with a token and an origin check, and the static files of `interface/` ("The local service" below). With `--messaging` it starts the messaging bridge as a child process. It imports `ops.py`, `shell_kit.py` (shared with the MCP mode), `service_bridge.py` (the child process) and nothing else of the runtime |
| `runtime/shell_kit.py` | What the local service and the MCP mode share, once: the project id and list, the mapping of an `OpsError` code to a status and a word, the argument kinds a shell carries in JSON and the job registry. It imports nothing of the runtime and holds no rule about the work |
| `runtime/mcp.py` | The MCP mode: the operations layer as tools of a Model Context Protocol server over standard input and output, one tool per row of the table that lists the `mcp` channel, with no token and no port ("The MCP mode" below). It imports `ops.py`, `shell_kit.py` and nothing else of the runtime |
| `runtime/service_bridge.py` | The child process (one per platform, several with a comma list) of `service.py --messaging <platform>[,<platform>]...`: the bridge's command line, its start in its own session with the pipe, the snapshot environment, its exit line and state, its stop (SIGTERM to its group, then SIGKILL after 150 s). Standard library only; the one file that names `runtime/messaging.py` |
| `runtime/messaging.py` and `runtime/messaging_*.py` | The messaging bridge: the operations layer reached from a messaging app with no model in between, as the channel `messaging`, one provider of the class `messenger:<platform>` per platform ("The messaging bridge" below). The shell imports `ops.py`, `shell_kit.py` and its own modules (cards, inbound, ledger, provider access, bridge) and nothing else of the runtime |
| `interface/` | The static files the service serves: pages, modules and vendored libraries, no build step, nothing loaded from another host. Its `demo/` folder (the demo mode: fictional data, no service) is never served; only the copy `scripts/build_demo.py` builds holds it |
| `runtime/flow_files.py` | Reads and checks a flow file, `flows/<name>.json` |
| `runtime/skill_meta.py` | What a skill declares in its frontmatter (artifact lists, requirement classes, side effects, version) |
| `runtime/path_rule.py` | The path rule: the one class of each path a run left (`state`, `machine`, `document`, `versioned`, `ignored`, `other`) |
| `runtime/state_merge.py` | The one module that decides what a run may change in `docs/workbench/state.md` (L10): of a run's text it takes a draft row of the skill that ran, a decision attributed to that skill and a new open question (read in any list form, written as a checkbox `- [ ] <text>`, refused when attributed to the person or to another skill), line by line, on top of what the project has now; the rest stays as the project has it, and each refused line is reported with its reason. `with_answer` writes the person's answer to a question as a decision of the user, and `with_go_ahead` the person's go-ahead on a task that waited; only code writes them |
| `runtime/endings.py` | The classifier of endings: a closed list; what no rule recognises is `unclassified` |
| `runtime/project_config.py` | The project's configuration, `docs/workbench/runtime.json`, and its hash; the optional `outbox` (EMAIL-SEND: `{provider, from}`, the account the project's e-mails leave from), the optional `accounts` (FND: where the platform may write in the person's own Google and Microsoft accounts: `{google|microsoft: {address, calendars, folders, mail_folders, move_removes_inbox}}`, names of lowercase letters, digits and hyphens, every id checked by a full-match pattern of its provider; `account_target` is the one place a write kind asks whether a target is named), `run_retention_days`, `source_versions_kept` (retention) and `sweep_orphan_containers` (P-OPS), and the refusal of `data_dir` and `store_db` in a synced folder; `default_model` (the model a request, a task and the router's run follow when nothing else chooses one; absent means `auto`) and `price_max_age_days` (default 90) are two of its keys; `language` (the language artifacts are written in: a tag matching `^[a-z]{2}(-[A-Z]{2})?$` such as `en` or `pt-BR`, absent means English; a wrong case is refused with the right form; `runtime/language.py` reads it for the line of a run and the plan card, "The language of a deliverable" below); `model_prices` is retired: still accepted, ignored, and `connections` warns and names the price table |
| `runtime/costs.py` | The cost of a run recomputed from its token counts (`usage_of`, `recompute`, `summary`) with the one price table (`runtime/prices.json`, through `runtime/prices.py`); a run without counts or a model without a price (or one lacking the price of a kind the run used) has no recomputed cost (`unknown`, counted), and a price older than `price_max_age_days` is shown as stale. It leaves at the change of the lab default list or of the grader (T23) |
| `runtime/prices.py`, `runtime/prices.json` | The one price table: for each model the four prices in dollars per million tokens, the URL they were read from and the day, and the other ids of the model (`aliases`). The cost of a run, the `auto` ranking (`expected_cost`), the proof page and the API adapter read it; `python3 scripts/update_prices.py` writes OpenRouter's prices into it from its models API, or, for a model whose gate entry has a `provider_pin`, from the endpoints API at the pinned provider (the dearest endpoint of the pin; never the cheapest endpoint of the model, never another provider's price), and an entry it did not write keeps the source and date it was read with |
| `runtime/instructions.py` | The person's standing instructions (limit L40): the file `docs/workbench/instructions.md`, its checks (UTF-8, a regular file, not a link and inside the project, at most 16 KiB read; the credential scan; no invisible character; no line of the form `--- ... ---`; at most 4,000 characters) and the block every model run receives in its prompt; stdlib only |
| `runtime/ops_contained.py` | The contained run ("The contained run" below), a part of the operations layer that `ops.py` exposes (`contained_run`); it reaches the helpers that stay in `ops.py` (`_route`, `_budget`, `_key_in_environment`, `_note`) at call time only |
| `runtime/language.py` | The language artifacts are written in (package LANGUAGE): the tag rule `^[a-z]{2}(-[A-Z]{2})?$` (the key, the request option, the router's line, the page and the store's column read it), the order that decides it (`effective`: the request's recorded language, else the key `language`, else `en`), the paragraph a task run and the router's run always get (`line`: `PROJECT_LINE` or `REQUESTED_LINE`), the plan's language line (`plan_line`, `payload_line`) and the requested language as a field of the plan's tasks (`with_language`). Pure, standard library only; the lab, the steps and a contained run never name it |
| `runtime/phrases_pt_br.py` | The Portuguese column of the phrase table and of its status words (package LR-1): data only, a pure literal; the one file written in Portuguese on purpose, so that the English-only check stays on every other file |
| `runtime/phrases.py` | The phrase table (package LR-1): `TABLE = {"en": {...}, "pt-BR": {...}}` (the English column here, the Portuguese one in `phrases_pt_br.py`), a pure literal of the sentences the runtime says to a person (the bridge's replies and cards, `/help` and the usage lines, the files and reminders sentences, the conversation's, the answer from sources), and `text(key, cfg, **fields)`, which picks the one of the project's language (`language.effective(None, cfg["language"])`; a tag with no table, and a key a table lacks, get English). Pure, standard library only |
| `runtime/ops_language.py` | The language in the operations layer: `checked` (the refusal of a bad tag on a request, code 2), `run_line` (the paragraph for the row of a task or a request), `of_plan`, `project_of` |
| `runtime/ops_instructions.py` | The operations over the file (`instructions`, `instructions-set`, `instructions-add`), the block of a task run, of the router's run and of a contained run, the check of a routine's task, and the proposal an answer may end with (`answer_proposing`, the row `answer`); a part of the operations layer that `ops.py` exposes |
| `runtime/ops_grants.py` | The effect, as the channel policy sees it (package POL-1): `effect_facts` (the word an `effect` decision carries and whether the whole of it can be on a messaging card, read from the effect document by the decision's own hash, through the kind's `on_cards`), `effect_kind` and `effect_refusal` (the sentence that refuses an effect to a channel outside `EFFECT_CHANNELS` unless the policy grants the word and it fits on a card); a part of the operations layer that `ops.approve` and `ops.pending` call |
| `runtime/policy_grants.py` | What `config` shows of the channel policy, pure (packages POL-1 and POL-2): the grants a configuration lists with what each gives away (a sender's profile's too), the warnings (a messaging effect grant while the notices carry no text, a profile nobody uses), and `view`, which joins them to the widening |
| `runtime/policy_widening.py` | What a configuration widens, pure (package POL-2): the record of what an accepted file effectively gives each place (a channel or a listed sender), kept in the store's cursor `config:effective` (a bit mask over `GROUPS` for each place written in base 64, versions 4 and 5 since S41-10; the hexadecimal versions 2 and 3 are still read; a sender only by a short hash of its key, which is no secret, at most 100 senders), and, since S41-10, the cursor `config:fallbacks` (the keyed digests of the fallback routes in force that the person accepted), the widening of a new file over it (any group a place did not effectively have, a grant or not), and the sha256 of that list that `accept-config --widen` takes; POL-1's cursor `config:grants` is read when the new record is absent |
| `runtime/ops_actor.py` | Who acted (package POL-2): the actor label the store keeps with a decision (`terminal`, `page`, `mcp`, `chat`, or `messaging:<platform>:<profile>:<sender id>`), the profile of a sender read from the configuration of the call, `guard` (the layer asks the policy again for the sender who acted) and `shown` (a pending decision as a shell reads it: `acted_by`, never the id); a part of the operations layer |
| `runtime/workcopy.py` | What enters a run copy, limits L1 to L6, each left-out file listed with its reason (`entering`), and what comes back, limits L7, L8, L12 and L14 (`returning`, `masked_reply`); the project's `AGENTS.md` enters only when the skill declares it, without the two lines the container cannot serve, and only its workbench section when a protected path covers it and the skill is not of a code area |
| `runtime/roles.py` | The skills the runtime is wired to (router, brief, sub-task), the code areas and the packs in use, as data: `runtime/roles.json`, checked against `skills/` and `packs/` |
| `runtime/isolated.py` | Skill code the runtime runs on the host (a document's checker, the backlog's task reader): an isolated interpreter with a scrubbed environment, never loaded into the runtime's process |
| `runtime/manifest.py` | A skill's runtime manifest, `skills/<name>/evals/runtime-manifest.json`, read and checked, with its optional `reply_phrases`; a task whose skill has no whole one does not run. The skills of the packs in use (`packs_in_use` of `runtime/roles.json`: `packs/business.txt`, `packs/brand.txt`, `packs/design.txt`, `packs/planning.txt` and `packs/code.txt`) have a whole one; another skill may have a partial one |
| `runtime/proof.py` | The proof of a skill and the choice of a model by it: a copy of what the status script computes (the lab's evidence and the person's own local tests), kept in `<data_dir>/proof.json` with one pair for each model of the gate file and rebuilt when its inputs change. A model the person names runs, whatever its band, and is released without review only where the skill is `reliable` on it, the measurement files are the recorded ones and the eval image is the evidence's; `auto` picks the cheapest `reliable` pair that has a key, a price and a measured usage (a tie to the higher pessimistic score; none: the project's default model, supervised); evidence from the community ranks a pair and never releases it |
| `runtime/sources.py` | The sources of a project: the registry, the immutable versions in the sources folder, the catalog built by code from the converted text, the guardrails of the folder (limits L21 to L27), the type sniffers it adds to the intake (PDF, Office Open XML, text), `import_version`, `recheck`, the copies a task receives (`task_copies`) and a Notion page read by its address. It imports nothing of the operations layer |
| `runtime/ops_sources.py` | The ingest operations (`source-add`, `source-import`, `source-refresh`, `source-describe`, `sources`) and the small pieces the rest of the layer asks of them: the catalog block of the router's prompt, the sources an `@mention` forces, the copies a task receives and the rows that record them. A sibling of `ops.py` |
| `runtime/attachments.py` | The files a listed sender sends through a messaging app (ATT-1): the limits (a file at most the intake's 25 MiB or the provider's own cap, 20 files and 100 MiB a UTC day for the project, 10 held, 5 a message, 7 days), the sentences a person reads, the checks made on what the platform declared before a byte is fetched, the name cleaner, the caption rule (only an exact `@<source id>` on one file is read), and the folders where a file waits (`<data_dir>/attachments/incoming/<token>/`, `held/<id>/original`, each made and opened through descriptors with `O_NOFOLLOW`, mode 0700; the file 0400). It imports the intake and the sources and nothing of the operations layer |
| `runtime/ops_attachments.py` | The operations over those files (`attachments`, `attachment-hold`, `attachment-add`, `attachment-discard`): the bridge's hold after a fetch (the intake bounds the file, reads its type from its bytes and hashes it; a declared type of the converter's list that the bytes contradict is refused; the file is recorded and kept), the add (the held bytes read back and hashed against the row, then `ops_sources.import_bytes` with a provenance that names the platform and the profile and never the sender) and the discard. A sibling of `ops.py` |
| `runtime/reminders.py` | Reminders, the pure part (RM-1): the words after `/remind` (`HH:MM`, `YYYY-MM-DD HH:MM`, `in <n>m\|h\|d`, a 24-hour clock with two digits, refused with the usage sentence when it can be read two ways) (the strict grammar: `strict` says whether a line begins with it, and a line that does not is read in the person's own words, NR-1) resolved against a clock and a zone (today or tomorrow, a wall time a clock change skips refused, a first occurrence for one that happens twice, at least one minute and at most 366 days ahead), the text rule (one line, 300 characters), the caps (100 waiting in a project, 30 a day for one chat), the late window (6 hours) and the lines a due reminder goes out as. No store, no clock of its own |
| `runtime/ops_reminders.py` | The operations over reminders (`remind`, `reminders`, `reminder-forget`, `reminder-restore`) and what the messaging bridge asks of the store to send them (`reminders_due`, which claims the reminders that are due, ends the late ones `missed` and the ones whose sender was removed or narrowed `dropped`; `reminders_sent`; `reminders_retry`): a stored one-shot notice with the person's own words, never an effect (limit L51). A sibling of `ops.py` |
| `runtime/messaging_remind.py` | The messaging bridge's part of reminders (RM-1): `/remind`, `/reminders` and `/forget` through the one call path, and the delivery of what is due at the end of each tick, to the chat that made the reminder and to no other. NR-1 adds the reply that shows the moment as a person reads it, the line in the person's own words (`natural`), and the question kept in memory for the next line of the chat (the draft: ten minutes, one per chat, never logged), with a one-tap Yes button where the platform has buttons (`Bridge.ask_yes`, `tapped`) |
| `runtime/messaging_guard.py` | What a phone never reads (LIVE-2): the finder of a shell command of this platform and of a path of this machine, and the net under the bridge's one send point that cuts them, adds one sentence and records an event |
| `runtime/messaging_outcome.py` | What an approved effect did, said to the chat in plain words (LIVE-2): the phrase family `OUT_`, from the decision's card and the effect module's closed facts |
| `runtime/turn_order.py` | The order in which a line is claimed, as data (S41-2): `ORDER` (a tuple of rows `{name, stage, what}`, and `channels` for the rows of the stage `ops`; stages `bridge`, `bridge-say` and `ops`), `FRAME_KINDS` (S41-8: the pending kinds the one row `frame` of the stage `bridge` dispatches over, in their order, and `kinds()`), `RETIRED` (a row that left, with its replacement and date: L63(3) says it in its row, and `runtime/tests/test_ops_turn.py` reads it there), `names(stage)` and `channels(name)`; pure, standard library only; the one source of the order of every claimer of a line (limits L63(3), L64 and L65 live in it) |
| `runtime/messaging_turn.py` | The bridge's dispatcher of `turn_order.ORDER` (S41-2): the code of every row (the conditions and calls `Bridge._handle` and `Bridge.say` had, copied), `Turn` (S41-8: with the envelope every kind of the frame reads, once) and `dispatch`, and `KINDS` and `frame`, the one dispatcher over the pending kinds (`applies` and `take` of each; `turn_order.FRAME_KINDS`); takes the bridge as an argument, reaches no operations module (layer map), changes no outcome (the entry replay) |
| `runtime/ops_turn.py` | The operations layer's dispatcher of the stage `ops` of `turn_order.ORDER` (S41-3): the code of every row (the conditions and calls `ops_say._turn` and `ops_natural.take` had, copied), `State`, `confirmed` (the OK of a plan), `dispatch` (the turn of a stored line, on every shell: a row whose `channels` excludes the shell is skipped), `code_claims` (the queue decision of `say`) and `dispatch_rows` (tests); reaches `ops_say` and the lookups at call time, changes no outcome (the entry replay) |
| `runtime/messaging_failure.py` | Why a write to the person's accounts was not made, said to a phone in plain words (LIVE-2): the phrase family `FAIL_`, by the closed code of a failed preparation or the words of a failed write |
| `runtime/natural.py` | Natural requests (packages NR-1 and NR-2; "Natural requests" below): `candidate` (what a line is, with no clock), `understand` and `complete` (the reminder a line asks for, the question asked back, and the answer to it), `describe` (a moment in the project's language), `question`, `made_sentence` and `forget_question` (the sentences), and, for every operation of the platform, `CLASSES` (the ONE literal that gives an operation its class), `NATURAL` (its slots), `match` (the closed tier-1 forms, language data), `needs`, `private`, `confirm_kind` and `check_operation` (the router's outcome believed against the line). Pure; the words are the language tables |
| `runtime/natural_time.py` | The time parser of natural requests (NR-1): a line in a person's own words to the grammar of `reminders.resolve` (`HH:MM`, `YYYY-MM-DD HH:MM`, `in <n>m\|h\|d`), or the readings to ask about, or a missing hour. Pure, with the clock passed in; it holds no word of any language: it reads every language table found in its folder and tries them all on every line |
| `runtime/natural_lang_*.py` | One language table each (NR-1): `natural_lang_en.py` and `natural_lang_pt_br.py`. Data only, a pure literal read with `ast.literal_eval` (never executed): the weekdays, months, relative days, "in N minutes" forms, hour markers, the words of a time not fully understood and the trigger words of remind, list, forget and calendar. Adding a language adds one such file, its name in `runtime/natural_languages.json` and its tests, never an edit of the engine; `natural_time.check_table` refuses a table that breaks the schema and a bridge checks them once at start. The Portuguese one is listed by name in `.security-scan-allow` for the `english-only` check, as `phrases_pt_br.py` is |
| `runtime/natural_writes_mail.py` | The mail family of the writes asked in words (NR-5b; "A mail write asked in words" under "Natural requests", limit L61): `read` (a line to `{op, slots, spans, need}`: a closed head, slots with closed markers, the person's own words as exact slices, an address only as written, a message by digits or by whole words), `answer` (the answer to the question a line left open), `match_words` (a message by the whole words of its sender and subject), `check_mail` (the shape of the data `requests.writes.mail`). Pure |
| `runtime/ops_natural_mail.py` | The resolution of a mail write asked in words (NR-5b): `resolve` turns what the family read into the arguments of the operation's own function or into the one question still open; the account, the mailbox, the message and the folder are chosen as the paragraph "A mail write asked in words" says; it opens nothing, writes nothing and imports no operation |
| `runtime/account_start.py` | The start of a write to the person's own accounts (package FND; limit L55): one operation prepares the change as one request with one step task, runs the preparation with no model, and stops at the card that asks for the approval of the hash of the exact document. It writes nothing, imports no effect module, no provider and no writer, refuses a channel the policy does not know and the MCP mode always, an account the project's `accounts` does not list, and a kind whose gate is not an account word. "A write to the person's own accounts" below |
| `runtime/calendar_document.py` | The document of a change to the person's own calendar (package W1; limits L55 and L56): the effect document (the account, the writer, the calendar by name and id, the operation, the event and the version of it the task read, the event as it was and as it will be, the zone, `sendUpdates` always `none`, the key and the dry-run hash), its checks (the limits of a title, a place, notes and a reminder, the credential scan, the forms of the times), the card line by line (`card_lines`, the keys `CARD_CAL_*`), the closed facts a decision records, and the arguments of the writer's verbs. It holds no function that calls a provider or executes an effect |
| `runtime/effect_calendar.py` | The effect kind of a change to the person's own calendar (package W1; the word `calendar`, limits L55 and L56): `execute` refuses an approval of another hash, reads the event again and compares its version (`stale`), checks that the writer's dry run hashes to the document's, and starts the writer's verb with `--confirmed`; `preflight` holds the account, its address, the calendar by name and id (`account_target`) and the writer's `SERVES`; `caller` starts the writer with the minimum environment and, for a real call, the one token of the account |
| `runtime/step_calendar_event.py` | The step kind `calendar-event` (package W1): prepare names the event (a reference), reads it through the read-only calendar service, refuses an event that is not the person's alone (organized by someone else, another attendee, a series, gone), asks the writer for a dry run and writes the effect document; the gate opens the `effect` decision whose card is the whole event. It imports `calendar_document` and `steps` and nothing that calls a provider (limit L30) |
| `runtime/ops_calendar.py` | The operations over the person's own calendar (package W1): `event-create`, `event-change`, `event-cancel`. Each one prepares and stops at a card through `account_start.start`; none writes. The group is `calendar-write`; the approval of the card is the grant `approve-effect-calendar` on a messaging app |
| `runtime/natural_writes.py` | A write asked in words (package NR-5): the registry of the families, the toolkit they read lines with, the recognition (`candidate`, `read`), `check_writes` and `problems`, a delegate to the gate's `turn_gate.value_problems` (S41-7: the fuzz's value oracle moved there, unchanged). Pure. Its family modules are `natural_writes_calendar.py` and `natural_writes_files.py` (and `natural_writes_mail.py`, package NR-5b), each with the closed heads and markers of its operations as data of the language tables |
| `runtime/ops_natural_writes.py` | The card-opening path of the natural writes (package NR-5, limit L61): the one place that opens a write for a line, in the order of the L54 dispatcher, then asks back what is missing (the draft of owner `write`) and calls the operation's own function; its family resolvers are `ops_natural_calendar.py` and `ops_natural_files.py` (and `ops_natural_mail.py`) |
| `runtime/ops_items.py` | The read `items` (package NR-5a): the numbered messages and events of the newest snapshots, with the rights of a reference |
| `runtime/ops_research.py` | The operation `research` (slice R2-F1; "A question answered by a web search" under "Natural requests"): one run of the skill `core-research` with web access for a question of general information, chosen by code, in the eval container with no file of the project; bounded by the key `research`; the group `research` |
| `runtime/research_reply.py` | The reply of a web research, written by code (slice R2-F1): the claims of the skill's brief in plain words, its sources as short links, cut to the limit of a message; pure |
| `runtime/turn_gate.py` | The gate of a person's line (S41-4, S41-5): the one home of the limits PLAN-BRAIN section 4.1 re-homes, one section each. Section 1: the daily route's admission (L63). Section 2, the line checks: the taint of R2-F1 (`research_allowed`: the closed possessive forms of a source of the project, "minha agenda", "my e-mails"..., in six languages, the bare noun allowed; it was `research_guard.py`), private routing (`private_line`: a reminder or a write in words never leaves the host, L51, L61, L63) and decision numbers from the line (`the_number`, `number_believed`: L54; it was `command_words.vet`). Section 3: one refresh budget per line (S41-6; L52, L53). Section 4, provenance (S41-7; L55, L60, L61): `ORIGINS` (the closed vocabulary: `line-span`, `line-number`, `time-engine`, `time-model` (a time the model read; allowed for a start and an end by the maintainer's decision of 2026-10-26: the card's approval is the protection, until a later v3 package adds time-engine tables for es/fr/de/it), `config`, `picked`, `listed`, `grounded`, `index-from`, `held`, `card-copy`, `composed`), `ALLOWED` (the origins each argument key may have: an address is never `grounded`, `listed`, `picked`, `held`, `time-engine` or `composed`; `composed` is the text of an e-mail only), `OPS` (the arguments each operation of a card opened from a line takes: a reply has no recipient), `provenance(op, args, origins)` (problems as key names and origin words, never a value; FAIL CLOSED: an operation, a key or an origin the table does not name, and an argument with no origin, each open no card), `Seed` (a seed with the origin of its parts, equal to the plain dict; a part a seed supplies and does not declare has none, `UNDECLARED`, and opens no card), `time_origin`, `origin_of`, `account_origin`, `refusal_line`, `refusal_text`, and `value_problems` (the fuzz's value oracle, moved unchanged from `natural_writes.problems`, which stays a delegate); every resolver and every seed says where each argument came from, and the card-opening paths ask before they open (`ops_natural_writes._opened`, `ops_file_trash._card`, `ops_event_revise.revise`); `runtime/tests/test_turn_gate_provenance.py`. Section 5, the fallback route (S41-10, L63): a second daily route of another company for a sender who holds `interpret-fallback`, one admission per line, a breaker for each route, the routes the newest entry's `fallback` names, each company shown to the person and recorded when they accept (`config:fallbacks`); off until the maintainer's dated entry names one |
| `runtime/registry_data.py` and `runtime/registry/` | The data the registry owns before PLAN-BRAIN step 2 (S41-9, decisions OPEN-41-10 and OPEN-41-11): `private_words.json` (the closed words and numbers of the private-line detector, `turn_gate.private_line`, limits L51, L61, L63) and `search_stop_words.json` (the search stop words, lead words, brands and calendar nouns of `event_lookup`), each with a header `{v, made_from, rule}`, and the pure loader `registry_data.py` (`private_words`, `search_stop_words`, `problems`; `--check`). Made once from the language tables by `scripts/registry_build.py` (deleted by S41-11), edited by hand under review after |
| `runtime/files_document.py` | The document of a write to the person's own Drive or OneDrive (package W4a): the effect document of a new file, a new revision or a saved file, its checks and the credential scan of its text, the exact requests the writer will send (its dry run) and their hash, the key, the card lines and the arguments of the writer's verbs. It holds nothing that calls a provider or executes an effect |
| `runtime/step_files.py` | The step kind `file-write` (package W4a): `prepare` checks the arguments, takes the address and the folder from `accounts`, reads the service through the read-only seam, takes the content and writes the document; the gate opens the `effect` decision whose hash is the document's and whose payload carries the card lines. It imports no module that calls a provider (limit L30) |
| `runtime/effect_files.py` | The effect kind `files` (package W4a): the one place a file is written to the person's Drive or OneDrive, reached only from `approve` with the approval of the document's hash; one of the four modules that may name the class `writer:files` (limit L58) |
| `runtime/ops_files.py` | The operations `file-create`, `file-replace` and `file-save` (package W4a): each prepares a card through `account_start.start` and writes nothing |
| `runtime/mail_reply_compose.py` | When the body of a reply describes its text and the model words it (BL7-2, an extension of slice S7; limits L60, L61, L63): `described` (a closed list of describing verbs and manner phrases; a body after a say verb or the literal marker is the text as written) and `text` (one call through `mail_compose.write(reply=True)`: the date, the language, an empty recipient line, an empty subject line and the person's own words, never the message answered; any failure leaves the person's own words, and the reply that carries the card then says so in one line, `MC_REPLY_NOT_WORDED`). It sends, approves and opens nothing. |
| `runtime/mailbox_document.py` | The document of a change to the person's own mailbox (package W3; limits L55 and L56): the effect document (the account, the writer, the action, the folder by name and id, the messages as they were read from the mailbox with the base each is checked against, or the draft; the zone, the reading time, the key and the dry-run hash), its checks (at most 20 messages, the places a move may go, the credential scan of a draft), the card line by line (`card_lines`, the keys `CARD_BOX_*`; the lines that show another person's subject and sender are `phrases.CARD_PRIVATE`), the closed facts a decision records, the spec of each writer verb and the structure the writer's dry run must have (`requests_match`) |
| `runtime/effect_mailbox.py` | The effect kind of a change to the person's own mailbox (package W3; the word `mailbox`, limits L55 and L56): `execute` refuses an approval of another hash, reads the state of every message and calls the card `stale` as a whole when any moved, checks that the writer's dry run hashes to the document's, and starts the writer's verb with `--confirmed`; a failure after the first message is `unknown` and says which positions are done; `preflight` holds the account, its address, a move's folder by name and id (`account_target`), `move_removes_inbox` and the writer's `SERVES`; `caller` starts the writer with the minimum environment and, for a real call, the one token of the account |
| `runtime/step_mailbox.py` | The step kind `mailbox-change` (package W3): prepare reads each message from the mailbox through the read-only mailbox service (its subject and sender now, its labels or changeKey and folder), refuses a message that is gone or already as asked, asks the writer for a dry run and writes the effect document; the gate opens the `effect` decision whose card lists every message. It imports `mailbox_document` and `steps` (and the address and e-mail checks) and nothing that calls a provider (limit L30) |
| `runtime/ops_mailbox.py` | The operations over the person's own mailbox (package W3): `mail-draft`, `mail-archive`, `mail-move`, `mail-mark`, `mail-trash`. Each resolves the references `<source>#<n>` with the caller's own right to read the snapshot (`item_refs`), then prepares and stops at a card through `account_start.start`; none writes, sends or deletes. The group is `mail-organize`; the approval of the card is the grant `approve-effect-mailbox` on a messaging app |
| `runtime/upkeep.py` | Retention and the dispatcher's upkeep (P-OPS): run folders older than `run_retention_days` (default 30) and the old version folders of each source beyond `source_versions_kept` (default 5, at least 2) except a version a task that is not finished received, and, unless the project's `sweep_orphan_containers` is false, the orphan containers in their conservative mode, and the held attachments (a file that waited 7 days is deleted and its row ends `expired`; an incoming folder older than 24 hours and a held folder no held row names are leftovers of a crash and are removed; limit L48), once an hour at most at the start of the dispatcher's round (limit L29); `python3 runtime/upkeep.py containers --include-running` is the explicit way to remove a stuck running run container. A sibling of `ops.py` |
| `runtime/orphans.py` | The sweep of orphan containers and networks of the eval harness, by the names `wb-eval-run-`, `wb-eval-keys-`, `wb-eval-model-` (a local model's server) and `wb-eval-held-` followed by 16 hexadecimal characters (the label alone selects nothing); it removes an ended run container, a key proxy or model server whose run container is gone and a network nothing of its run is on, and never a running container unless asked; the harness's shared proxy, networks and key holders are never selected; `tool` is the container client (the lab facade's `container_tool`; a test passes a fake) |
| `runtime/lease.py` | The lease of the sources folder (`.openhora-lease`: host id, name, pid, expiry; limit L28), taken right before a write, the host id kept in `<data_dir>/host-id`, and `synced_reason`: a folder taken as synced, where `data_dir` and `store_db` are refused |
| `runtime/secret_state.py` | Whether the OS secret store is locked or unavailable, for the messages of the runtime: a probe with a name nothing stores, kept 30 seconds; a locked store is not a missing secret (finding I3) |
| `providers/converter/` | The provider of the class `converter:document` (runtime only): PDF, Word, spreadsheet, presentation, CSV, HTML and text to Markdown or CSV by poppler's `pdftotext`, pandoc, openpyxl and python-pptx (presentations) in a container with no network ("The sources" below) |
| `providers/script/` | The provider of the class `script:project` (runtime only): runs one project script in a sandboxed container, builds its dependencies from a hashed lock file in a container with no mount, and gives a step that declares destinations an internal network and a per-step egress proxy ("Project scripts" below) |
| `runtime/step_project_script.py` | The step kind `project-script` (not unattended): the script, its destinations and its lock file approved by their sha256 in the plan's arguments, run by the provider, its result a new version of a source |
| `runtime/ops_models.py` | Which model a run goes to, in the operations layer: the request's, the task's, the agent's fixed model, the project's `default_model`, else `auto`; the key found for it (the runtime's own, `WB_RUNTIME_KEY_<variable of the model's credential>`, which wins when stored, else the lab's), the billing of its credential, and whether the agent's consent (decision D38) makes the run `consent` instead of `supervised` |
| `runtime/router.py` | The router step: what code reads of one run of the router skill asked only for the route (the route line, or the question lines); anything else is unclassified and reaches the person whole |
| `runtime/drafts.py` | The one store of the questions a line leaves open (NR-2): a reminder waiting for its "yes" or its time, a reminder to forget, the choice of a file for a question; memory only, one per channel and sender, ten minutes where the owner sets a limit, at most fifty, never in a file or a log. Both the messaging bridge and the operations layer read and write it |
| `runtime/ops_natural.py` | The one dispatcher of the natural requests (NR-2, limit L54): the order of checks (row, channel, `operations.refusal` with the channel and the sender, the class), the reads written by code, the drafts (a reminder waiting for its "yes", a question waiting for the choice of a file), and `ask` with the person's whole line. On a messaging app (S20) a command with a typed form is run by that typed line (`hand_over`), and `approve` is never run from words. A sibling of `ops_say.py`, which calls it; the order in which a line is taken (what `take` was) is the table of `turn_order.py`, dispatched by `ops_turn.py` |
| `runtime/command_words.py` | The words that choose the project the lines go to (S20): a pure reading of one line, in the six languages the interpreter reads; the messaging bridge does what the typed `/project` does. A sibling of `natural.py`, which it uses to fold a line |
| `runtime/source_resolve.py` | A file named in the person's words (NR-2): which source of the project a line means, by plain rules on its name, id and description with the aliases of a kind of document; code only, no model |
| `runtime/plan.py` | The plan of a request (L19): the tasks code builds from a flow file or a route, the skills in scope, the hash the person approves, an estimate; several deliveries of one request, a brief routed again, the sub-tasks a backlog proposes; the pure derivation of the waits between requests (`derive`, `skill_facts`, `required_inputs`, "Derived waits") |
| `runtime/board.py` | The mirror of tasks with the project's task board (`integration:issue-tracker`): only code talks to the board; an item's note says what its task waits for, and an `after #<n>` in an item's words is the override "after request #n" |
| `runtime/documents.py` | The mirror of documents with the project's documents platform (`integration:documents`): the documents a runtime manifest lists; a page that changed is never written over, and an edit is imported only on the rules of the module |
| `runtime/drop.py` | The file drop: a file the person hands to one task, in `.workbench-local/drop/<task id>/`, enters that task's runs only; the folder is opened one component at a time with `O_NOFOLLOW` (limit L2) |
| `runtime/deps.py` | A project's dependencies, installed by code in the eval image with no model, cached, and copied into the run copies that need them |
| `runtime/changeset.py` | The change set of a code task (L9, L11): what a run did to versioned files, taken by git in the run's container and checked by the host |
| `runtime/effects.py` | A skill with a confirmation gate runs up to it (L16); the payload it showed is recovered, and code executes the effect once the person approves its hash (L15, L17). The one registry of the kinds of effect, keyed by the words of the side-effect vocabulary (`create` -> `effect_pull_request`, `push` -> `effect_commit`, `publish` -> `effect_reply`): the gate path reads the word a manifest names in its gate, the policy path the `kind` of a handler's effect document, and both find the same module. A kind module exposes `PROVIDER_CLASS`, `describe(doc)` and `GATE` (true when a skill's gate may name its word; a registered kind that is not `GATE` opens a review there, never an effect); for the gate path `parse`, `verify`, `execute` and the rest `effect_pull_request.py` lists; for the policy path `POLICY` (true when it may run under a standing approval), `policy_platform(doc)`, `policy_effect(doc)` (its target, the resolved one, is the one the action records) and `policy_argv(doc)`, and two optional names: `resolve_targets(cfg, doc, resolve_call)` (for a kind whose bounds name a class of targets: the set of targets the class holds now, read from a recorded fact through a provider's read-only verb, before the write lock) and `policy_judgement(root, project, doc, run_script, policy_file, log=None)` (the checks a kind owns beyond the bounds, on the exact content, after `covers` and before any provider call). A kind is a module and a row of the registry; `ops.py` names none. **The word never picks the kind alone** (R4-C6): `create` means a pull request for the pull-request skill and something made in a design tool for the design skills, so for the gate path `effects.gate_kind(word, requires)` returns the module only when it is `GATE` and the skill declares, among its `requires`, the module's `PROVIDER_CLASS` (`integration:vcs` for the pull request). A skill that declares another class (`integration:design-tool`, `generator:image`) or none names no kind: its task needs no `code` in the configuration, nothing is parsed as a pull request, no effect is opened, and a reply that reaches its gate opens a review for the person to read; the skill's own gate text says what it asks of its tool, and the runtime has no kind that executes it. A code skill's `create` stays the pull request, with its refusals (`code` configured, a change set to show) and its approval by hash. The effect of a skill is never opened for a class the skill did not declare, so the change can only turn an effect into a review, never the other way (`runtime/tests/test_gate_kind_by_requirement.py`). `EFFECT_KEYS` and `RESERVED_FLAGS` (the common shape of a handed-over document and the flags only the operation adds) are in this module |
| `runtime/effect_pull_request.py` | The kind `create` (a gate kind, `POLICY` false): the pull-request skill's payload form, its effect document, the checks, and the execution through the code provider |
| `runtime/effect_commit.py` | The kind `push` (a policy kind): a commit of files to a branch by the code provider under a standing approval; it has no gate-path function, since no skill's gate names the word |
| `runtime/effect_reply.py` | The kind `publish` (a policy kind, `GATE` false: a manifest gate that names `publish` still opens a review): a reply to a comment on one of the project's own posts, by a publisher (`publisher:<platform>`, the verb `comment`) under the engagement policy's standing approval. It reads its document (the publisher's four flags, each once, the four files of the judgement), resolves the target class from the publisher's ledger (`resolve_targets`: the read-only verb `posts`, the post ids read back from the addresses by the platform's template) and judges the exact text (`policy_judgement`: the file is the one the document hashed, the credential scan, and `mkt-engage`'s `policy_gate.py decide` through `runtime/isolated.py`, which must answer `auto` with the document's idempotency key) |
| `runtime/billing.py` | How the credential of a run is billed (`subscription`, `metered` or `free`), read from the adapters' manifests and nothing else (`contracts/secrets.md`); it decides which daily cap a run counts against ("The daily caps") |
| `runtime/autonomy.py` | The five autonomy modes of an area agent, what each releases, the daily caps by the billing of the credential a run uses, and whether a standing approval covers an effect: a word of a bounds' `targets` that is a class (`TARGET_CLASSES`, today `comment-on-published-post`) covers a target only when the operation resolved it before the call (`covers(..., resolved=)`; no resolution, or an empty one, covers nothing); and the bounds of a Markdown engagement policy, derived from its ```engagement-policy block (`block_bounds`) |
| `runtime/progress.py` | Progress and the summary of a period, computed from the store's records |
| `runtime/dispatcher.py` | The dispatcher's decision (`decide`, pure; a task with an open wait never starts) and the scheduler's entry for the two jobs ("The dispatcher's two jobs" below) |
| `runtime/chat.py` | The conversation with the planning agent in the terminal, one more shell of `ops.py`: its commands and its help are the table's |
| `runtime/handlers/` | The handlers a round of the dispatcher ticks: `published_posts.py` (the weekly routine that lists the published posts), `social.py` (the social agent), `social_vote.py` (its weekly vote step, imported by `social.py`) and `social_vote_job.py` (the scheduled publication of the vote post, started by the scheduler only). A handler prepares an effect and hands it to the operations layer (`cli.py execute-under-policy`); it confirms no provider verb, re-reads no bound and records no action (L15). The social agent's auto reply follows that rule since CONS-2a: `social.py` hands the reply to `execute-under-policy` ("The social agent's auto reply" below) and calls neither the gate nor the publisher on that path. The social files still differ from the rule in the paths that confirm an exact content the person approved by its hash, which code checks (`EXACT_CONTENT_PORTS` in `runtime/tests/test_runtime_rules.py`, by function: `cmd_approve` of `social.py`, the vote post's approval and record in `social_vote.py`, and the vote job `social_vote_job.py`), and in the skill scripts they run as host subprocesses (the tolerated edges of `scripts/tests/test_layer_map.py`: the notification parser and the vote step's scripts), and `social.py` loads `providers/resolve.py` and `scripts/redact.py` with its own loader (`load_resolution`, `load_redact`); the package that moves those onto the operation and removes the exemption is CONS-2b, after the launch |
| `runtime/tests/` | The tests of the above, offline, with a stand-in adapter and invented skills; `corpus/` holds the classifier's corpus of archived lab runs |
| `flows/*.json` | The flow files: `market-positioning.json` (a market analysis, then the customer profile and positioning), `brand.json` (the brand flow), `code-change.json` (the code-change flow), `design.json` (the specification, the UX flows, the design system and the briefs of five screens) and `website.json` (a website, from its PRD to a static site: the specification, the site map, the design system, one brief, the code prototypes, the hand-off and the build); a project's own flows are `docs/workbench/flows/*.json` ("Flows" below) |
| `providers/store/sqlite.py` | The store (class `store:runtime`): migration 2 holds the task runtime's three tables, migration 3 adds `task_runs.redactions`, migration 4 a task's item on the task board, the document records and the saved platform comments, migration 5 the approvals table, migration 6 the conversation's messages, migration 7 the triggers that refuse to delete a task, a run or a pending decision (L13), migration 8 the derived waits between requests (`tasks.after_request`, the table `task_waits`), and migration 9 `task_runs.billing`, how the credential a run used is billed, and migration 18 the table `attachments` (a file a messaging app brought, from the hold to its decision) and `source_versions.provenance` (ATT-1), migration 19 the type `onedrive` of the sources table (O2), and migration 20 the table `reminders` (a one-shot notice for the messaging bridge, RM-1; not in the export), and migration 21 the types `mail` and `calendar` of the sources table (RT-1); its functions are the contract, one transaction each |
| `providers/documents/` | The documents provider (`integration:documents`): `notion.py`, with its pure converter `notion_blocks.py`, and the stand-in `local.py` |

The configuration of a project names four absolute paths: `workbench` (the checkout the runtime runs from, a reviewed revision), `data_dir` (a folder outside every repository, for run folders and the locks), `store_db` and `sources_dir` (the sources folder, mandatory with no default: a project without it does not run; limits L21 to L27, "The sources" below). Every operation refuses a project whose configuration names another checkout than the one it runs from, and a configuration whose hash is not the one the person accepted last (`accept-config --sha256 <hash>`, kept in the store).

**The configuration, and who may accept a change of it.** A change of `runtime.json` takes effect only when its hash is the accepted one, and the person accepts a hash in the terminal (`accept-config`, recorded as `by: "person"` in the store's cursor `config:accepted-by`). One exception, for a change that can only take power away: `set-mode`, from any channel (the terminal, the page), to a mode lower in the order `stopped < supervised < milestones < autonomous < autonomous-with-policy` (`runtime/autonomy.py`, `MODES`; `narrows(old, new)`) is a narrowing, and code accepts the new hash at once, as `accept-config` would (the same cursor, the state file's `Checkpoints` line rewritten), recorded as `by: "code:narrowing"`; nothing refuses afterwards. It does so only when the configuration was the accepted one before the call (every operation checks it first) and the file read back is the one the call wrote. A move up the order, or any other change (a cap, an agent, a pack, a handler, a price, a credential), is never accepted by code: the hash stays unaccepted, every operation answers `412`, and `set-mode` returns `accepted: false` and `next`, the command that accepts it. One command does both steps for a move up, and only for the person in the terminal: `set-mode --accept` sets the mode and accepts the hash it makes in the same locked step, recorded `by: "person"` (as `accept-config` records it), and returns `accepted: true`, `by: "person"`, `next: null`. It does so on one condition, checked under the lock: the configuration's hash was the accepted one before the change, so the only difference it accepts is the mode it names. The hash it accepts is that of the exact bytes it wrote (taken before the file is replaced), and the file read back must still have it: an edit that lands after the write is not accepted. Any other state (another change in the file nobody accepted, or a file or an accepted hash that moved since the call began) is refused with the sentence of `accept-config` (exit code 3, or 1 for a call that lost a race) and nothing is written: the person reads the file and runs `accept-config`. A narrowing with `--accept` behaves as without it (`code:narrowing`). `accept` is the terminal's alone: the operation refuses it (exit 2, before it reads anything) from every channel but `terminal` (`ops_config.ACCEPT_CHANNELS`), the page's route takes the mode and nothing else (a body with `accept` is a `400`), and the conversation and the MCP mode have no `set-mode`; so a page can never accept, and can never widen anything that is then in force. The commands the page offers to copy for a wider mode (`wider` of `agents`) carry `--accept`, so Copy gives one command that works. Two changes never overlap: `set-mode` and `accept-config` take a lock (`<data_dir>/config.lock`, from the service's threads and from the terminal alike), and under it `set-mode` reads the file and the accepted hash again; when either moved since the call began (another request finished first) it writes nothing and is refused, so a stale copy of the file can never bring a widening that code then accepts. A dispatcher round likewise ends when the file or the accepted hash moved during it, so a narrowing stops the next start. The single writer of the accepted hash is one function (`ops_config._record_acceptance`), called by `accept_config` and by the one branch of `set_mode` that the test `narrowing or accept` opens, and by nothing else; a test over every pair of modes and tests of the call graph keep it so (`runtime/tests/test_mode_narrowing.py`, `runtime/tests/test_set_mode_accept.py`). The same mode changes nothing and writes nothing.

**The commands the runtime names.** Every sentence the operations layer or the service returns that names a terminal command (the `412` refusal, `set-mode`'s `next`, the commands of the held reasons) is built by `operations.command_line`, the one place that spells the syntax. It is absolute (`python3 <this checkout>/runtime/cli.py ...`), so it runs from any folder, and it carries the prefix `uv run --with keyring==25.7.0` when the process was started through uv (uv sets `UV_RUN_RECURSION_DEPTH` for what `uv run` starts, whatever environment it made; failing that the interpreter lives in uv's cache or under `UV_CACHE_DIR`; `WORKBENCH_STARTED_WITH_UV` set to `1` or `0` says it outright) or when the operation needs the secret store (`run-next`). A text stored before it is shown, such as the body of an effect, names no checkout and keeps the relative form.

**The social agent's auto reply.** The reply to a comment is an effect under a policy, so it is executed by `ops.execute_under_policy` like the published-posts commit, and by nothing else. The handler (`runtime/handlers/social.py`) runs the agent as a contained run, takes its decision, and writes into the run's folder the reply text, the comment, the sources the agent cited and its category and language; it builds one effect document (policy `engagement-policy`, kind `publish`, the post as the target, the four files, one item, the key the gate derives from the comment's identifier, the hash of the reply, and the publisher's own flags) and hands it to `cli.py execute-under-policy`. It passes no `--confirmed`, runs no gate and records no action. On `executed` it appends the engagement log entry and the event is `done`; otherwise the reply goes to the inbox with the operation's reason among its reasons, as an item the person can approve by hand (the exact-content path, unchanged). Two conditions hold at once, and neither alone sends anything:

1. *The bound, in code.* The standing approval of `docs/marketing/engagement-policy.md` carries bounds that `approve-policy` derives from the file's ```engagement-policy block, not from a bounds file: effect `publish`, target class `comment-on-published-post`, no files, one item per run, and the block's `max_replies_per_day` as the daily cap (a block without it is refused at approval; a Markdown policy without the block keeps the three keys it had and covers no effect). The file's hash binds the block, so a changed block covers nothing until it is approved again, and the bounds are derived again then. A class word is no literal target: `autonomy.covers` accepts the document's target only when it is in the set the operation resolved before the call, from the publisher's ledger through its read-only verb `posts` (it reads the ledger and nothing else, with no token and no request). So "a post this project published" is a fact code recorded and not a reading of the notification, whose text anyone can write; a ledger that cannot be read, lists no such post or answers nothing makes the reply not covered, and nothing runs. The post id the action records is the one the ledger matched.
2. *The judgement, by the skill.* After `covers` and before any provider call the operation runs the kind's judgement: the parent comment is a comment on the post of the request (the platform's data file gives the form of a comment id, which embeds its post, because the publisher addresses the request to the parent), the run folder is `runs/<n>` of the project's data folder, the text file is read once and its bytes are the ones the document hashed, they pass the credential scan (R4, `scripts/redact.py`'s formats), and are written to a private file (a folder the operation makes, mode 0700, the file created exclusively, 0600) which the gate reads and which the operation gives the publisher in place of `--text-file`, so the text sent is the text judged whatever happens to the run folder's file afterwards; the private folder is removed when the call ends. Then `mkt-engage`'s `policy_gate.py decide` (category, language, the daily and per-person limits, sensitive topics, the reply rules, the sources), started through `runtime/isolated.py`, answers `auto` with the document's idempotency key, run at the operation's clock (`--now`, the operation's local time with its offset; the zone the gate computes a day in is still the machine's, since the gate runs with a bare environment); otherwise the gate's reasons are the `why`. The gate's `auto` is never the approval: the standing approval is.

The operation then makes the publisher's dry run and its confirmed call and records the action (kind `engagement-policy`, target the post). Cut-over on a project that already runs the agent, in this order and by the person: `approve-policy --file docs/marketing/engagement-policy.md --agent <the area agent in autonomous-with-policy> --expires <date>` (an approval given before the derivation carries three keys and covers nothing), `standing --policy engagement-policy` must answer `covered: true`, then `python3 runtime/handlers/social.py replay --project <dir> --runs <data_dir>/runs` judges the stored runs again through the operation with `--replay-log` (every check, no execution, no record; the log it reads is a copy cut before the run, so that a run the old path sent is not refused for having been sent): every run the old gate sent must be `auto` and every run it held `inbox`, and a difference is read before the switch, never after. Only then does the scheduler's tick run this code. A tick whose reply is not covered writes an inbox item, never only a log line. The operation takes the project's write lock (it waits up to 60 seconds for the other writers of the project's files and effects, and runs beside task runs, which hold no lock while they work): a reply that finds the files busy for longer is refused ("the project's files stayed busy for 60 s; try again"), and goes to the inbox with a `failed` log entry and a `to_inbox` one. The replay does not apply the run-folder rule (it judges a scratch copy of the stored files and executes nothing); if the replay says a run the old path sent is not covered because "the target is not one of the comment-on-published-post the operation resolved", the ledger records another URN type than the notification carries, and a mapping the provider supplies is needed before the switch, never one invented here.

## A task run

Several tasks of a project run at once (decision of 2026-10-12, `docs/decisions.md`). `run-next` is the person's verb: it takes the oldest `ready` task that the rules below let in and runs its skill once, in its own process.
