The file is also withheld, with the same sentence, when it cannot be read to the end: a package with an embedding, a macro project, an encrypted part, a name that leaves the package, or any part that is neither text nor a picture; a PDF that is encrypted, whose Flate stream does not inflate to its end, whose stream has a filter or a predictor the module does not read, or that is cut by a cap; a converter that fails; any error. **The gaps, stated:** a credential drawn inside a picture is not seen, nor is one written in a picture's metadata, such as a PNG text chunk (an image is sent as it is; the images of an Office package under its media folder, and the image streams of a PDF, are not scanned), and a PDF stream compressed with an image codec (DCT and the like) is not read; a credential that a PDF writer splits across text operators is seen only when the converter's text joins it. **Nothing is stored:** the bytes live in memory for the call and are handed to the bridge in `result["attachment"]` (never to the service, the page or the terminal: only a messaging call keeps them), the bridge writes them to one temporary file of a new private folder for the length of the send and removes the folder in a `finally`, the conversation keeps the neutral word of S8, and no log names a file or a folder.

**A question answered by a web search (slice R2-F1; `runtime/ops_research.py`, `runtime/research_reply.py`; the group `research`).** A line such as "how will the weather be in Sao Paulo tomorrow?" (the live line of 2026-10-22 was in Portuguese) or "search tomorrow's forecast for Sao Paulo" is the class R operation `research` (slot `question`, a span of the line): the one read that calls a model, picked by the interpreter for a question of general information (the weather, a price or a quote, the news, a fact) that no file or source of the person's own answers. **What code decides, before anything runs:** (1) the sender's profile holds the group `research` (in no default policy; valid on a messaging app and in the terminal, which is never narrowed; no row for the page or the MCP mode), else the plain refusal of any group (L62) and nothing runs; accepting a file that gives it needs `accept-config --widen`, whose list prints the line "answers general questions (weather, prices, news, facts) from a web search: the question is sent to a web search service and to the model that runs the research, and the answer comes back to this chat"; (2) the skill is the one the code names (`core-research`), a folder of this checkout that declares `search:web` and no side effect, else the sentence "the web search is not available in this installation"; it is outside every area agent's pack and needs no runtime manifest, because nothing it writes comes back; (3) the model and adapter by the rule of every conversation run (the project's `default_model`, else `auto`: the cheapest pair on which the skill is `reliable`, else the reference model), and the web only when the chosen pair was MEASURED with the web (the proof's `web`, "The choice of the model"): a pair that was not runs nothing and the reply says there is no web, so proof decides what the web is used for; (3b) the question: one that holds a possessive form of a source of the project ("minha agenda", "my e-mails", "mon dossier"; `turn_gate.research_allowed`, `runtime/turn_gate.py` section 2, six languages, the bare noun allowed) is not run, and the person is asked to say the line again; (4) the bounds of the project's key `research`: `max_per_day` runs in a local day (default 10; counted from the store, only runs on a request of the skill, so a task of a flow or an agent that runs the same skill is not counted; a failed run counts; the terminal's and a phone's together), and per run `max_cost_usd` (default 0.60, the adapter's `--max-cost-usd`, always given) and `timeout_seconds` (default 300), one attempt, with the route lock held (one conversation model call per project at a time; a busy lock is its own sentence, RS_BUSY). **The run:** `ops_contained.execute` with `open_web`: the eval container on the open network (the adapter's `--allow-web`, no change to any adapter or to a fingerprinted file) and NO file of the project in the copy, not the state file, not an earlier brief; its prompt is the question in the person's words, then a fixed task text that names the consumer (the chat) and asks for a short brief, and nothing else: NOT the person's standing instructions, which every other model run receives, because a page the run reads could ask it to send out what it was given (a run on the open network gets nothing of the project, in its files or in its prompt). No router run, no plan and no question to the person precede it; the stage `research` is published for the acknowledgement of S19 ("Pesquisando..."). Nothing the run writes comes back. **The reply** (`research_reply.py`): written by code from the brief in the copy, never the skill's own form: at most six claims in plain words (an estimate, an opinion or a low confidence says so; a claim that is a slash line, a whole card word of `runtime/card_words.py` or card-like is dropped), the sources the claims cite as short links (at most five, tracking dropped, a link only when it is http or https, all printable ASCII, with a host that is not an address, a local name or a user name), cut to the limit of a message with the sources never cut, one sentence when the run is not proven, no skill, flow or file name and no code fence; a failure is one plain sentence (no web, a timeout, the cost cap, which also says to try again or to raise `research.max_cost_usd` and accept the configuration again, the daily limit, nothing established, the question to say again, any other); the section "Instructions found in external content" of the skill is never read. **What is kept:** the question is the person's line, which the interpreter stores as a neutral word (L63), and the reply is stored as a neutral word too (RS_STORED), so the conversation, which the MCP mode reads, holds neither; the run is a row of the store as an answer run is (a request called "Question <n>" whose text is the neutral word "web research", and its run with the skill, the model, the tokens and the cost), which the daily count and `costs` read; the store holds neither the question nor the answer; the run folder (`<data_dir>/contained-runs/<n>/`) holds the prompt and the brief for `run_retention_days`, like every contained run, outside git; nothing is logged or printed (an error prints the name of its type only). **Where code cannot enforce it:** whether the interpreter picks `research` only for a general question: the catalog's words say it is not about the person's own files, mail, calendar or sources, but the interpreter's corpus has no item of the operation yet (`PENDING_CORPUS`), so a line about the person's own data that the model sends to `research` is believed when its span is the line's, and its words go to the search service; the protection is the group, which is off until the person widens it, and the corpus items that the next revision of the corpus adds; the sources of the answer are the skill's and a page it read may be wrong. The run, the model and the web are the lab's; `core-research` is `needs a test` today, so a research answer carries the sentence that it is unproven until its full test passes. **The brake by words (BL2a, 2026-10-24).** On a messaging app a search that runs is recorded in memory for the sender who asked it (a `pending_items` item of kind `research-run`, the request id only), and that sender's stop word (`plan_confirm.stop_form`; the messaging bridge reads it under L64's conditions) cancels it through `ops_research.stop`, the cancel path that stops what runs; a sender who does not hold `research`, another sender and a request that is not a search are never reached, and the search's reply is `RS_CANCELLED`. The default cost cap and its message are the research section's own (`RESEARCH_DEFAULTS`, `RS_CAP`).

**An item named by its words (B″-lite, 2026-10-24; `runtime/event_lookup.py` `_by_target`, `runtime/natural_writes_mail.py` `target_words`, `runtime/ops_natural_mail.py` `mail_target_seed`; limit L61).** The operations that act on an item that already exists take, besides the number they always took, the words that name it: `event-change` and `event-cancel` take `target` (the event's name as the line writes it) and `day`; `mail-reply`, `mail-archive`, `mail-move`, `mail-mark` and `mail-trash` take `target` (a sender or a subject) and `day`; `mail-draft` takes `target`. The number slots stay (`event`, `message`, `messages`) and a number written in the line is still the exact form, so the rule that a decision number must be written in the line is unchanged; a line that names the item by its words gets `target` and `day`, spans of the line the check verified (a span that is not a piece of the line is a clarify, and an `inherit` is taken only from the last line of the same operation). Code grounds them with the search that already exists: for an event, the closest name over the rows of the newest calendar snapshot and the events this platform created (the calendar is read again once on a miss, and only for a sender who may read a snapshot); for a message, the words of the target must all be whole words of the from-address or the subject in the index of the newest mail snapshot, after the right to read it was checked, and the `day` keeps the messages the index says came in on that local day (a weekday: within the last 7 days; an index with no `received`, or a day the time engine cannot read, such as "yesterday", is the sentence that a message is not found by its date); a file is searched by its `file` span, as before. The results are the same everywhere: one match is the card (with the code-rendered sentence when the name was near), several matches are a numbered list and "o 2" picks one, no match is one plain sentence. A project whose language has no tables (es, fr, de, it) keeps the sentence that says so for these two families. With no `day` span the day is the one in the target's own words, else the window search, and the rest of the line is never read for a day ("cancel the dentist, but not tomorrow's" names no day of the event). With a `day` span the day is read from two places (BL5C, 2026-10-25): the span and the rest of the line (the line with the target, day, time and title spans cut out); the same day twice is that day, two different days are the question EV_ASK_WHICH_DAY, which names both and asks for the event and the day again (nothing is opened or kept), and a span that names no day (the model took a word of the title for it) takes only a date WRITTEN in the rest (a slash or numeric date, never a day word), else EV_ASK_DAY (a rest that names another object, such as a mail, is ignored). The time of an `event-change` is read with the event's current day (a `day` span that is a date and nothing else, before a time span that holds a day of its own, and not inside the target) left out of the line, and a time span may leave out the trigger word the time engine puts before a phrase ("para sexta às 10h" read from "sexta às 10h"); a `day` with no `target` and no `event`, for a change or a removal, is asked again; a removal has no time slot, so a `time_iso` the model adds to an `event-cancel` is dropped and never read. A date with a time range on one day ("25/10 13:00-14:00") names that day, and a time range on several days is asked; no shape the time engine can answer ends a line in an exception. Nothing here approves or executes: every path ends in `open_line`, and the card is approved by its hash.

## The text contract

An adapter may declare `"text_runner"` in its `adapter.json` (a script in its folder) and `"text_files"` (the files of the runner, relative to the folder). The runner is for a caller that wants the reply of a model to a system text and a user text, exactly, and for nothing else: it runs no tool and keeps no session. It exists for the tests of the natural-language interpreter on a model that runs on the person's own account (package ACCT-A, `docs/decisions.md`); the runtime's daily path never calls it. `<runner> --model <id>` reads one JSON object on standard input and prints one JSON object on standard output:

```text
in : {"v": 1, "system": <text>, "user": <text>, "max_tokens": <int 1..64000>, "reasoning": "off|low|medium|high|xhigh|max", "deadline_ms": <int 1..600000>}
out: {"v": 1, "text": <reply>, "finish": "stop"|"length"|"other", "usage": {"input", "output", "cache_read", "cache_write"},
      "latency_ms": <int>, "runner_version": <text>}
```

The request has exactly those keys. `system` is the same for every call of a prompt and holds no line of a person; `user` is the one place the line goes, and it reaches the harness through its standard input, never through its argument list. `usage` is the harness's own count (tokens, not money); `latency_ms` is the wall time of the call from the start of the harness to its end, not counting the start of the runner. `finish` is `length` when the reply stopped on `max_tokens`. `runner_version` names the runner and the harness's own version, so that an update of either shows.

**Failure.** One line on standard error, `run-text: E_<CODE>`, and nothing else; standard output stays empty. The codes are the interpreter evidence's (`evals/evidence_lines.py`, `IX_ERRORS`): `E_USAGE` (exit 2: a request that is not exactly the one above, an option the runner does not take); `E_KEY` (exit 3: the credential is missing or the harness says it is not valid); `E_ROUTE` (exit 3); `E_TIMEOUT` (the deadline passed); `E_RATE` (the harness says the account's limit is reached, by the words its `eval.json` lists in `account_limit`, read and never changed); `E_NETWORK`; `E_UPSTREAM`; `E_RESPONSE` (the harness answered without a reply or without its token counts); `E_INTERNAL` (anything else); all exit 1. The harness's own output is read in memory: it is never forwarded, so a failure never carries a word of the system text, the line or the reply.

**What the runner writes, and the time it takes.** The runner writes neither the system text, the line nor the reply. It makes one temporary root of mode 700 in a fixed folder of its own, `/tmp/run-text-<uid>` (never the caller's temporary folder), with the harness's working folder (empty), configuration folder, temp folder and home folder, and removes the root when the call ends, however it ends. The harness is told to keep no session; what it writes anyway outside the root is not prevented by the runner, and is exactly what the probe lists (`files_outside_root`, below). The harness is started in a process group of its own with an environment of a closed list of names (the credential and the harness's own switches; nothing else of the caller's), and a process that moves itself into a session of its own escapes the group. One monotonic deadline starts when the request is read and covers the harness's version check and the call; at the deadline the group gets TERM and, a moment later, KILL, the pipes are given a second and then closed, and the runner returns within `deadline_ms` plus 3 seconds whatever a descendant still holds. When the harness returns, what it left running in its group ends too. A caller that stops the runner sends TERM first: the runner then ends the harness's group and removes its root. After KILL the runner cannot clean: its root stays, and the harness, in a session of its own, runs until it ends; the next call of the same user removes roots of the fixed folder older than the longest deadline plus a minute. The credential is read from the runner's own environment, put there by the caller; the runner never reads a secret store.

**The probe.** `<runner> --probe --model <id> --max-tokens <n> --reasoning <r> [--repeat N]` runs the very same command against a stub that listens on the loopback address only, with a placeholder in place of the credential and probe texts in place of a system text and a line, each carrying a fresh random nonce of fixed length (16 hexadecimal digits) that is the one thing a file is searched for. It makes no model call and needs no credential. The probe texts are the hard cases of a line: the line has leading and trailing spaces and newlines, several lines, CRLF, a letter with a combining mark, non-ASCII, lines that start with `/`, `!`, `#` and `@`, an e-mail address and a quote; the system text has several lines and non-ASCII. It prints one JSON object:

- `runner_version`; `runner_sha256`, the sha256 over the files of `text_files` (for each, in sorted name order, its name, a newline, its bytes, a newline).
- `envelope_sha256`, the sha256 of the canonical JSON (sorted keys, no spaces, UTF-8) of `{"beta": <the sorted values of the request header that lists the API's beta features>, "body": <the request body of the one messages call>}` with the values that vary from run to run replaced first (the root of the run, in each form the harness may write it, by `<ROOT>`; a date, a time and the sentence that gives today's date, by `<DATE>`), then the probe system text by `<SYSTEM>`, the probe user text by `<USER>`, any nonce left standing alone (a harness that echoes part of the line) by `<NONCE>`, and `metadata.user_id` dropped. `replaced` says how many of each were replaced (`{"root", "date", "nonce"}`), so an echo is a named cause and not an unexplained mismatch; the characters of `extras` are counted after the replacement.
- `system_verbatim` and `user_verbatim`: true when exactly one text block of the request is the probe text, whole (no trimming, no change of line ends, no recomposed letter), and the text occurs once in the whole request.
- `messages_requests` (the number of calls to the messages path); `paths` (every path the stub was asked, with its count); `extras` (every other text block, by place and number of characters, and the tools if any); `sent` (`max_tokens`, `temperature`, `thinking`, `effort` as the request carried them); `stable` (true when the `--repeat` runs sent the same thing); `overhead_ms` (`p50`, `p95` of the harness's wall time against the instant stub).
- `reply_ok`: true when the runner's reading of the harness's output, in every run, is the stub's answer, `finish` `stop` and the stub's token counts (so a change in how the harness reports its reply shows before a route is written).
- `credential_ok`: true when the stub saw no credential but the placeholder.
- `files_outside_root`: the files and folders created or changed during the probe outside the root, in the places the harness is known to write (a folder of the user under the system temporary folder, the system temporary folder and the caller's, the harness's own folders and files in the person's home), **only those that are flagged** (a file that holds the probe's nonce, or a path under the probe's own folder), with path, time, `probe_text` and `own_folder`; `outside_other_count`: how many other new files there were, counted and never named, because the folders of other sessions can carry a person's project names. A file's content is read only to look for the nonce, whole (in chunks that overlap, so a nonce across the edge of two is found) and as ASCII and as UTF-16 in both byte orders. `outside_unread_count`: how many files could not be read whole, and how many entries or folders could not be looked at (a permission error is one; only an entry that has vanished is passed over), counted and never named. `outside_protected_count`: how many folders the operating system refused to list with its privacy protection (errno EPERM; on macOS the system temporary folder's `TemporaryItems` is one, on every run), counted and never named; such a folder is kept from the harness the runner starts too, since both are the same user, so it does not leave the scan incomplete and does not count as unread (a refusal with EACCES, errno 13, and every other listing error stay in `outside_unread_count`). `outside_depth_limited_count`: how many folders changed during the probe that the depth limit stopped at and no other place listed, counted and never named. The harness's own places (its folder of the user under the system temporary folder, and its entries in the person's home) are walked with no depth limit, bounded by a number of entries and a time that, when spent, leave the scan incomplete; the general temporary folders (the system's and the caller's) are walked to a depth of two. The limit that remains: a new file two or more folders below the depth limit, under folders that existed before the probe, is not seen in the general temporary folders. `outside_scan_complete`: false when a scan limit stopped it, when any file was unread, or when any folder was left unlisted; `outside_ok` can then not be true. A busy machine shows files that are not the harness's (other sessions of it write in the same places), so the flagged entries are published with the count of the others, and the rule that decides is `outside_ok`: true when the scan was complete, no file holds the probe's nonce (a fresh one for each probe, so the runner's own source, and the log of a session that quotes it, hold the templates and never a nonce), and no listed path is under the probe's own folder (the root's random name, or the name the harness derives from the working folder's path by turning every character that is not a letter or a digit into a dash). A new, unrelated file in the same places does not fail it.

A route that uses the runner is written only when both texts are verbatim, `messages_requests` is 1, `stable`, `reply_ok` and `credential_ok` are true, `outside_ok` is true (the flagged entries and the count of the others are published with the band); text the harness adds is accepted only when it is listed in `extras`, recorded in `docs/decisions.md` and published beside the band (principle 10). A caller that holds a route compares `runner_version`, `runner_sha256` and `envelope_sha256` with the route before it spends the credential: any difference is `E_ROUTE`.

**What the probe does not show.** A copy of the nonce that is transformed (base64, compressed, encrypted, split) is not detected by the scan of files outside the root; nor is a file that a limit of the scan left out, which the scan says (`outside_scan_complete`). The probe is not the real call. It adds to the environment the address of the stub, the placeholder credential and the names of a closed local proxy (five names the real call does not have); its texts are fixed, short and not a person's line or a catalog; its token counts and reply are the stub's; and what the harness does with a real credential (a login check, a profile read, a keychain read, an extra request for an account) is not seen at all, because the stub answers a request for them with an empty reply and the proxy closes every other way out. A route written from a probe says so: the first real call of the account runs under the same envelope check only for what the stub could see.

## Records

**Three tables** of migration 2 of the store: `tasks` (a request, with no parent and no skill, and the tasks of its plan), `task_runs` (one row per run: skill, version and content hash, model and adapter, web, status, failure kind, ending, attempts, cost, tokens, duration, whether the skill was reported loaded, image digest, run folder, error, and `billing`, how the credential the run used is billed, "The daily caps") and `pending_decisions` (what a task waits for the person to decide: kind, title, the reply as the body, a payload, status, resolution and answer).

**Nine task states:** `requested`, `planned`, `ready`, `running`, `waiting`, `blocked`, `done`, `failed`, `cancelled`. A `waiting` task always has exactly one open pending decision; several tasks of a database may be `running` at once; which may is decided by the runtime under its claim lock, not by the store.

**Cancel and stop at any moment (R18, limit L41).** Two verbs, both in the groups `brakes` (and `cancel` in `reject-cancel`), on the terminal, the conversation, the page and messaging (where they are a grant of `channels.messaging`: the group `brakes`), never on the MCP mode: `cancel --request <id>` ends a request and everything under it, `stop-task --task <id>` ends one running task and nothing else. `runtime/ops_cancel.py` does both, in this order: (1) it takes the request's effect lock and keeps it for the whole call (limit L41); (2) it writes the **stop request first**, in one transaction, holding the project's write lock (`tasks.stop_requested_at`, `tasks.stop_requested_by`, migration 15: on a request row "cancel this request", on a task row "stop this task"): every task of the request that is not running and not final is cancelled at once, with its open pending decisions and a revoked approval of an effect still waiting to execute, so nothing new is claimed and nothing waits at a gate; (3) it ends what runs: the holder of each running task's lock gets TERM (`run_stop.stop_tasks`: its group when it leads it, never this process, never a lock nobody holds), the lab's own handler ends the containers of the run, the lock is waited for `run_stop.STOP_GRACE` (30 s), the same holder then gets KILL, and whatever the process tree named before TERM (`ps`, the `--name wb-eval-run-<id>`, `wb-eval-keys-<id>` and `wb-eval-held-<id>` of the lab's `docker run` lines, exactly 16 hexadecimal characters) and is still there is removed through the container tool, each shared object checked again before its removal (`orphans.remove_runs`); the router's run happens inside the calling process and is never signalled: a watcher thread beside its lab call (`run_stop.watch`) reads the request's stop request every second and ends the lab's runs of that process, and the canceller waits `run_stop.ROUTER_WAIT` (30 s) for the run row to end, finishing it itself when the route lock is free (its owner is dead); (4) what a dead holder left is finished under the store's rule. **A run that is stopped ends `cancelled`** (`task_runs.status`: `running`, `ok`, `failed`, `cancelled`; `failure` and `ending` null; `error` "cancelled by <who>", where who is "user from <channel>" or "board"; the tokens and cost its attempts reported, read with `lab.reported_usage`, as for a failed run). A run that finishes after the stop request is recorded `cancelled` whatever it found, opens no pending decision, and returns nothing into the project; a finish that committed before keeps its result. The task ends `cancelled` for a cancel; for a stop it ends `blocked` with the note "stopped by <who>: retry it or cancel the request", so that `retry` makes it ready (and clears the stop request), a step task resumes at its step, its dependents stay `planned`, and the request and its other tasks go on (a `cancelled` task is final and would leave the request stuck: `_refresh` waits for every dependency to be `done`). The request is `cancelled` once none of its tasks and none of its runs, the router's included, is running. **An output being written** (NOTION-OUT): the approval of an output holds the request's effect lock and the lock of its decision for as long as the write takes. A cancel that has a running task to signal is refused then (L41), nothing stopped; a cancel with nothing to signal goes on and, as every way of cancelling a request does (`ops_cancel.request_cancel`, `cancel_request`), leaves open the decision whose lock is held and the task it belongs to, cancels everything else and the request, and says so (`writing`, `said`, `kept`); the request remembers what it kept (the cursor `stop-keep:<request>`), so that its end, which may come from a run's finish, keeps it too. **Spend:** a `cancelled` run keeps its reservation and counts as a run that may have spent (`autonomy.never_reached_the_model` is false for it); the caps, `progress` (a `cancelled` count in the summary), `_reported` and the page read it as any ended run. **Idempotent and bounded:** a second call finds the request written, writes nothing new, repeats the stop and answers `{"request", "cancelled", "stopped": [{"task" or "run", "state": "cancelled" | "stopping"}], "pending", "already": true}`; `stopping` is a run that did not end within the waits, and its owner or the next recovery (`task_fail_orphans`, which ends a run whose request has a stop request `cancelled`, not `failed`) finishes it. A request already `cancelled` answers `already` and is no error. A routine's request that is cancelled changes nothing of the routine (`routine-cancel` is its own verb); the runtime brake (`set-mode --agent runtime --mode stopped`) still only stops new starts. The page asks to confirm a cancel while something runs ("what it has used so far is spent, and nothing it made is kept") and gives a running task a `Stop task` with the same question; `POST /projects/{p}/tasks/{id}/stop` is its route.

**Six endings** of a run that did not fail: `done`, `question`, `draft_with_questions`, `gate`, `blocked`, `unclassified`. Every ending is recognised by a rule that reads only the run's facts, what the skill declares (its frontmatter and runtime manifest) and sentences of the skills' own text; what no rule recognises is `unclassified` and reaches the person whole. The rules are tested against a corpus of archived lab runs, with labels a person read (`runtime/tests/corpus/`). `blocked` (the reply names a missing input and the skill that writes it) opens nothing: the task is `blocked` until the person retries it, and its `note`, in `status` and in `task`, is the first line of the reply that names the missing input and the skill that writes it (`endings.missing_input_line`; the start of the reply when no line does), so that the person sees what unblocks it beside Retry. `gate` (the run wrote the file the manifest names for its confirmation gate and asks) opens an `effect` when its payload was recovered and parsed, agrees with the configuration and its change set is not blocked; otherwise a `review` whose body starts with the reason (`runtime/ops.py`, `_effect_or_review`). A run that changed no file, asks nothing and finds the task's declared output already in the project is `done`. A run that failed has a failure kind instead: `timeout`, `refused`, `auth`, `adapter`, `early_end`, `settings`, `stopped` or `internal`.

**Six kinds of pending decision:** `plan` (stage 3), `question`, `review`, `effect` (stage 4), `acceptance` (stages 3 and 6) and `your_document` (stage 10). The store accepts all six; the runtime opens five today, and `your_document` is not opened yet:

| Kind | Opened when | Resolutions |
|---|---|---|
| `plan` | The router's run on a request gave a route, or the person named a flow (`route --flow`): code built the plan, with its hash | `approve --sha256 <hash>`: the plan's tasks are created and those with no dependency and no wait are `ready` ("Derived waits"; `--go-ahead <task key>` drops the waits of a task); `reject`: the request is cancelled |
| `question` | A run ended `question`: it wrote nothing (a change limited to the state file counts as nothing) and asks | `answered`: the task is `ready`, and its next run gets the answer |
| `review` | A run ended `done`, `draft_with_questions` or `unclassified`, or ended `gate` without a payload that makes an `effect`: the body is the whole reply, so a draft's open questions are in it | `released`: the task is `done`, the tasks that depended on it become `ready`, and the document stays a draft with its open questions; `answered`: the task is `ready` again, and its next run gets the person's text |
| `effect` | A run ended `gate` and its payload was recovered, parsed, agrees with the configuration and its change set is not blocked; its hash is that of the effect document | `approve --sha256 <hash>`: the approval is recorded (L17), code checks that nothing moved and executes the effect (L15), and the task is `done`; `answered`: the task runs again with the answer; `rejected`: the task is cancelled and nothing is sent. Answering or rejecting revokes the approval in the same transaction |
| `acceptance` | An item a person wrote on the task board became a request; a backlog proposed sub-tasks outside the approved plan's limits (`subtasks`); a delivery was routed again after its brief, or a request an autonomy mode completed waits for the person's acceptance of its deliveries (`deliveries`) | `accepted` or `rejected`: a board request accepted is routed next, rejected is cancelled; accepted sub-tasks are added to the request; for deliveries the resolution and its note are recorded |

## Derived waits

A person adds requests in any order: on the page, in the terminal or on a task board. The runtime orders the tasks by itself, from the artifact contract (`contracts/project-layout.md`): what a task needs is the `inputs` its skill declares, and what an open task will write is the `outputs` of its skill. A task that is not started waits for another task, and the wait is stored with its reason.

**The rule** (`runtime/plan.py`, `derive`, a pure function; `runtime/ops_waits.py` reads the store and the project's files and applies it). For each task that is `planned` or `ready`, and each path of its skill's declared inputs that does not exist in the project (a path with placeholders is a pattern; a path that ends in `/` is a folder with a file in it): the owner skill of the path is the skill whose declared outputs hold it; if a task of another open request has that skill, the task waits for the oldest such task, with the reason `<path>, written by task #<id>`. Then:

- An input the project has creates no wait. An input that a task of the task's own request writes is the flow file's business (`depends_on`), not a wait. An input nothing writes creates no wait. A skill that reads its own output (a plan it keeps up) waits for no task of its own skill and reports nothing missing for that path: it starts it.
- A task that waits stays `planned` and the word is *waiting for task #n*; the dispatcher never starts it (`dispatcher.decide` holds a ready task with an open wait as `waiting`; the store's `task_claim` refuses it, so the rule does not rest on the caller). The wait ends when the awaited task is `done` or `cancelled` (the store clears it in the transaction that moved the task), when the file appears (the next derivation), or when the runtime no longer derives it (a request cancelled, another writer found). A task whose waits all ended becomes `ready`. An awaited task that failed or is blocked keeps the wait: the task waits for it until it is retried or cancelled.
- A wait that would close a circle with the dependencies and the waits already made is not made and is reported (the plan's `cycles`); the older task keeps its wait. A derivation never waits in a circle.
- A skill's **required** input is a row of its `## Inputs` table whose Required cell, lowercased, is exactly `yes` (one reader, `flow_files.required_inputs`, the one `scripts/validate.py` uses for the flow files and `plan.skill_facts` calls; `**yes**`, `yes.` and "yes for a person" are not the word, and a table whose header has no Artifact and Required columns is not read). A required input that the project does not have and no open task writes is said before the person approves a plan: `<skill> needs <path>; nothing writes it: run <owner> first or go ahead and it will stop` (`add the file` when no skill owns the path). It is a sentence, not a refusal: the plan can be approved, and the task will stop on the missing input.

**The override.** `request --after <id>` (the terminal, the page's request form, `/new --after <id> <text>` in the conversation, an `after #<n>` in the title or text of an item on the task board) keeps the request `<id>` on the new request (`tasks.after_request`; a task's id means its request). Every task of the request waits until that request is `done` or `cancelled`, with the reason `after request #<id>`. It is a wait the contract cannot see; a circle of `after`s is reported like any other. **Go ahead:** `approve --id <plan> --go-ahead <task key>` (repeated, or a list in the page's JSON) removes the derived waits of those tasks of the plan, in the transaction that creates them, and keeps the decision: the dropped wait stays in `task_waits` as `dropped`, and a later derivation does not bring back that wait, the same path for the same awaited task. The person agreed to the waits they were shown, not to every wait for ever: a writer that replaces the one they went ahead of (a cancelled request made again) or another path is a new wait. Code writes one line to `## Decisions` of the state file as the person's decision (`state_merge.with_go_ahead`). Go ahead leaves the `after` override alone. A key the plan does not have, or that waits for no task to derive, is refused and the plan stays open, naming the key. **Go ahead on a task already created:** `go-ahead --task <id>` (the terminal and the page, `POST /tasks/{id}/go-ahead`; not the conversation, not the MCP mode) ends the derived waits of that one task the same way (`dropped`, one line in `## Decisions`) and returns `{task_id, state, dropped, waiting_for, decision}`. It is refused for a request, for a task that is not `planned` or `ready`, and for a task with no derived wait, which includes one that waits only for the request it was told to follow: an `after` is the person's own, and only they drop it, for one task, with `--drop-after` (the page sends `drop_after: true`), recorded the same way; the override of the whole request is changed by `request_after_set`, which a board item does when the words `after #<n>` are added to, or taken out of, its text.

**When it is derived.** When a request is planned (`request` with a flow, `approve` of a plan: the waits of the new tasks are written with the tasks, in the same transaction, so that no task is ever `ready` before its wait exists); when a task ends or a request is cancelled (`release`, `reject` of an effect, `cancel`, `retry`, and the store itself clears a wait whose awaited row is `done` or `cancelled`); when a board item is accepted; at the start of every `poll` and every round of `dispatch`, and before a task is claimed, which is how a file that appears on its own is noticed. A derivation is cheap when there is nothing to derive: with one open request and no `after`, the skills are not read. Everything a derivation changes is one `waits_sync` of the store: it opens the waits that are derived and not open, clears those that are not derived any more, and sends a `ready` task that gained a wait back to `planned`.

**What the reads carry.** `status`: each task of `requests[].tasks[]` has `waiting_for: [{task_id, request_id, reason}]` (empty when none; `task_id` is the awaited task, `null` for an `after` wait, whose `request_id` is the awaited request), and each request has `after` (the request it runs after, or `null`). `task`: the same `waiting_for` in the task row, and `after` for a request. A pending decision of kind `plan` carries, before approval, `waits: [{task_key, task_id, reason}]`, `missing: [{task_key, skill, path, owner, sentence}]` and `cycles: [{task_key, sentence}]` in its payload, and the same lines in its body above the plan hash; the plan hash covers the tasks only and does not move. `approve` accepts `go_ahead` (the table's `list` kind: repeated `--go-ahead` in the terminal, a JSON list on the page; the conversation cannot carry it) and returns `waits` and, for a go-ahead, `state` (`{"written": true}`). `request` returns `waits` and `missing` for a flow. A mirrored item's note on the task board says `waiting for #10, #12 (+1)` (at most two awaited tasks named, the rest counted, a request as `request #n`) before the task's own note. On the board an `after #<n>` is an instruction only at the start of a text or on a line of its own; inside a sentence it is prose. `run-next` with nothing ready names the tasks that wait and why (`no task is ready; waiting: task 12 for ...`).

**The store.** Migration 8 adds `tasks.after_request` and the table `task_waits` (`task_id`, `awaited_id`, `kind` = `input` | `after`, `path`, `reason`, `status` = `open` | `cleared` | `dropped`, `created_at`, `ended_at`, `ended_by`); a wait is never deleted (L13, a trigger), it ends. `waits_list`, `waits_sync`, `waits_go_ahead` and `request_after_set` are its functions. `request_add`, `plan_approve`, `tasks_add`, `acceptance_resolve` (accepted sub-tasks) and `task_retry` take the waits their tasks begin with, in their own transaction, so that no task is ready before its wait exists; the one exception is a retry the task board asks for during `sync`, which `sync` derives right after the pull.

**Known limits.** A plan that is approved before the request it depends on is planned gets no wait (that task is not there yet): the plan says the input is not written, and the next derivation orders them, but the task may have started in the meantime. The owner of a path is the owner skill only; a skill that merely updates a file (`updates`) is not a writer. A `yes` of a Required cell that carries a condition is not read as required.

## The daily caps

Package ADJ-R3 (row A-38 of the interface adjustments, decided 2026-10-10). An area agent has two caps, `max_runs_per_day` and `max_usd_per_day` (an absent cap is 0, never unlimited). Which one a run counts against follows the **billing of the credential the run uses**, not the model:

| Billing of the credential | A run on it | Counted against | Reason when held |
|---------------------------|-------------|-----------------|------------------|
| `subscription` | is covered by a fixed fee | `max_runs_per_day`, one per run | `cap: runs per day` |
| `free` | costs nothing (a local model, no key) | `max_runs_per_day`, one per run | `cap: runs per day` |
| `metered` | is paid by use (an API key) | `max_usd_per_day`, in dollars | `cap: usd per day` |
| `unknown` | started on a route no manifest could bill; written by the runtime, never declared | `max_usd_per_day`, as a metered run (a reservation, its dollars are never left uncounted) | `cap: usd per day` |

**Where the billing comes from.** Each adapter's manifest states it for every credential it registers, and `login_billing` for a harness that holds a login of its own (`contracts/secrets.md`, "How a credential is billed"); `runtime/billing.py` reads them and nothing else. A run's credential is what `lab.run_skill` passes: the variables the gate file names for the model (`models[<id>].pass_env`), the runtime's own key for the model travelling under the first of them; a model with no variable uses the adapter's `login_billing`. Several variables are billed by the strictest word (`metered`, then `subscription`, then `free`). A credential no manifest lists, or an adapter that declares no login for a run that passes no variable, has **no billing the runtime can know**: the dispatcher starts no task on that route (`may_start` refuses with `unknown billing None`, which a page reads as the reason `other`; a start by hand, `run-next`, is not capped, as it never was) and a run that is made all the same (a start by hand) records the word `unknown`, which counts as `metered`. A row with no billing at all (NULL) is only a row older than migration 9.

**An API key as a model's credential** (`ANTHROPIC_API_KEY` under the first lab model's harness) is today an edit of that model's `pass_env` in the gate file that nothing else supports: the caps count those runs in dollars, but the key enters the container with `-e` and is not held by a key proxy, so limit L6 does not hold for it. A route for it is a later package (A-40). `login_billing` is reachable only in a configuration that passes no variable; in the container the CLI's login does not exist.

**The cap counts the runs in progress, and is checked at the start under the claim lock.** Runs overlap now, so a run row that is still `running` counts in the day as soon as it exists (a subscription or free run as one run, a metered run at its recorded cost or its reservation), and the row is inserted under the claim lock in the same step as the check: two processes that start at once cannot both pass the cap. Within one round the dispatcher also counts each task it decides to start as one more run of its agent's day before it asks about the next.

**The cap is checked at the start, and a metered run carries its own limit.** `may_start` holds a metered task when what is left of `max_usd_per_day` is under `MIN_RUN_BUDGET_USD` (0.05, `runtime/autonomy.py`) with `cap: usd per day`, so no run starts with a budget of a few cents. A run that does start is given a spend limit, `autonomy.run_budget`: the per-run limit (`max_cost_usd_per_run`, else 0.5) or what is left of the day's cap rounded down to the cent, whichever is less, computed before the run's own row exists and passed through `lab.run_skill(max_cost=)` to the adapter's `--max-cost-usd` (an adapter that enforces it stops the run at it; one that cannot says so, and the provider key's own credit limit is its bound). A subscription or free run is given none. A run that fails or times out after the model may have been called keeps its reservation counted: its recorded cost, else `max_cost_usd_per_run`; one that never reached the model reserves nothing ("The rule" below). A start by hand (`run-next`) is not held by the cap but carries the same limit, or the per-run limit when less than the floor is left. The router's run and a contained run on a metered credential carry one too.

**The run records it.** `_route` (`runtime/ops.py`) adds `billing` to the routing of a task run, of the router's run and of a contained run; the row of `task_runs` gets it when the run starts (migration 9: `ALTER TABLE task_runs ADD COLUMN billing TEXT`, a CHECK of the three words or null), and the `task` read carries it on each run row. A run row older than the column has none: for history only, `autonomy.history_billing` gives it the rule the caps had before, by model (a run on a model whose credential is declared `metered` is `metered`, any other `subscription`), so a day that began before the update counts as it always did.

**The cost follows the billing.** The run's `cost_usd` is the adapter's number (`timing.json`) when the run is `metered`, whatever its model, and null otherwise: a subscription or free run has no billed cost, and `runtime/costs.py` still recomputes one from the tokens and the price table (`runtime/prices.json`) for the report. A metered run whose cost is not recorded yet counts at `max_cost_usd_per_run` (else the default), and an unknown price never blocks a run: the reservation stays counted.

**The rule** (`runtime/autonomy.py`, pure). `spend_split(runs, agent, per_run_usd)` counts, from the day's rows with their billing: `runs_counted` (subscription and free runs), `usd_recorded` and `usd_reserved` (metered runs, the second for a run of unknown cost), `runs_without_cost` and `runs_total`; `spend` folds it into `runs_counted` and `usd_metered`. **A run that never reached the model reserves nothing** (A-57, P-19): a run row whose stored failure proves that no model was called is counted in `runs_total` (the page lists it) and in nothing else: no reserved dollar, no `runs_without_cost`, and no place in `runs_counted`, since it used none of the credential's allowance (two refused keys must not lock a subscription agent out of its day, nor fill a dollar cap). The proof is `autonomy.never_reached_the_model(run)`, from one definition of the words (`autonomy.NO_SPEND_FAILURES`): `auth` (the provider refused the key or the credential, a 401; set by the lab's attempt loop, `evals/run_attempts.py`, never retried) and `settings` (the copy carries a tool's settings; refused before the adapter is called), each only for a run whose `attempts` is 1, and `internal` with `attempts` 0, the runtime's own word for a refusal before the adapter (the eval image missing, a credential not set, a bad configuration, a copy that could not be built, a task refused before its run: `ops._run`, `ops._router_call`). The word alone is not the proof: with retries an earlier attempt may have spent before the one that was refused (`attempts` 2 or more), an adapter may meet a 401 after several calls, and the lab reads "HTTP 401" from what the run left, which includes the model's own words; so a run that reported any tokens or any cost is never one, whatever its word. A run's row, failed or ended, stores what every one of its attempts' `timing.json` reported (`lab.reported_usage`: the tokens summed over the attempts, the retried ones included, the paused ones too since a paused attempt may have spent before it stopped, and the cost on a metered credential), so a run that succeeds after failed attempts records the spend of all of them and not the last one's alone, and a failed run with a recorded cost counts that cost and is no reservation. The folders it reads are the run's own: run ids are never reused (`task_runs.id` is AUTOINCREMENT), so a leftover run folder is never read as another run's. `runs_since` reads `attempts` (the attempts that began; the lab names them on an exception that leaves it, `lab.run_skill`, so a crash after an attempt began is `internal` with `attempts` 1 or more and is not one) and `tokens`. An attempt that stopped on the account limit is a pause and counts as no result for `attempts`: the lab takes it back off the count and the run starts again from its beginning (its reported usage is still counted). A copy that fails inside the lab (`LabError("copy")`: the prepare or finish hook of an attempt) has `attempts` 1 or more and keeps its reservation, because the attempt had begun and the hook may run after the model; a copy that fails before the lab is called (`workcopy.CopyError` in `ops._run`) is `internal` with `attempts` 0 and reserves nothing. A row that records a cost is never one: what was billed is counted. Every other word keeps the reservation and the place in the cap, because the run may have spent and dollars are never left uncounted: `timeout`, `refused` (the provider declined a request it received), `adapter` (it failed after it started), `early_end` (the model answered), `stopped` (a restart or a stop of the lab, mid-run) and `internal` with an attempt. Runs of the day that were recorded `internal` with `attempts` 0 before a crash mid-run could be told apart (the day of the change) are read as refusals before the adapter. `may_start(name, agents, facts, spent, billing)` takes the billing of the run about to start: `subscription` and `free` against `max_runs_per_day`, `metered` against `max_usd_per_day` (the sum rounded to 6 places), any other word refuses. The dispatcher's snapshot carries `billing` for each ready task beside its `model` (the model decides the credential check), and `decide` passes it.

**What the reads carry.** `agents` keeps its keys: `runs_today` is the runs counted against `max_runs_per_day` (subscription and free), `usd_today` the dollars counted against `max_usd_per_day` (metered), with `usd_recorded`, `usd_reserved`, `runs_without_cost` and `runs_total_today` as before; it adds `billing` (`{"runs": ["subscription", "free"], "spend": ["metered"]}`, the words each counter counts) and `caps_in_use` (`{"runs": bool, "spend": bool}`: true when a run of the day, or a model the runtime can run on (the first lab model's always, another's when a key for it was found), has a credential billed that way). A page draws a meter only for a cap in use, and both when it gets none of the two keys (an older service). The caps of `costs` carry the day's use in the same words as before.

## The choice of the model

Package P3 of the delegation platform plan (decisions D1, D3, D4, D5, D8, D22, D34, D36, D38). There are no tiers: a skill has a band for each pair of model and adapter, and a run goes to the model that the first of these names (`runtime/ops_models.py`, `resolve`): the person's `--model` for this run (`run-next`, `run-task`), the task's own model, the model of the request the task belongs to, the area agent's fixed model, the project's `default_model`, else `auto`. A model is an id (or an alias) of `evals/eval-gate.json`, or the word `auto`; the store keeps it in the `model` column of the request or the task (migration 10, NULL for a row older than the column).

- **A model the person names runs.** Its band informs and ranks; it does not gate the choice. A pair that is not `reliable` on this machine (the band, the measurement files, the eval image) is unproven: the run is recorded `autonomy: "supervised"` (the `autonomy` column of `task_runs`, migration 10) and its review waits for the person, whatever the agent's mode.
- **`auto`** picks, among the pairs where the skill is `reliable`, a key for the model was found, its price is known (`runtime/prices.json`) and its with-skill runs measured a usage, the one with the lowest expected cost per run (the mean usage by kind times the price of each kind); a tie goes to the higher pessimistic score; for a routine the provider must be inside the approved set. No candidate: the project's default model when it names one, else the first lab model, supervised, with the reasons in the routing. Evidence of the lab and of the person's own local tests ranks and releases; community evidence ranks only, so a pair `reliable` through it alone is chosen and its output is reviewed.
- **Release.** `autonomy.review_action` releases a review only for a run recorded `proven` (and still proven on the model that ran, read now) or `consent`. A run is `proven` when the pair is `reliable` by the lab's or the local evidence; it is `consent` only when its model is the agent's fixed model, the agent is in an autonomous mode, and the consent stored with the agent names that model and that mode. External effects keep the hard limit: the approval of their exact content, whatever the consent.
- **Consent (D38).** The agent's settings in `area_agents.<agent>` hold `model` (an id, or `auto`) and `consent` (`{"model", "mode"}`). Fixing a model id on an agent in `autonomous` or `autonomous-with-policy`, or raising an agent with a model fixed to one of those modes, shows `autonomy.CONSENT_WARNING` and is given in one of two ways. In the terminal: `--consent`, a flag of its own and distinct from `--accept` (which accepts the hash), on `set-model` and `set-mode`; without it the call is refused with the warning and the command, and with it the answer carries the warning and the terminal prints it. On the page: the operation `give-consent` (`POST /projects/{p}/agents/{name}/consent`, channel `page` only), whose body is the model, the mode, `config_sha256` (the hash of the configuration the page read) and `warning_sha256` (the sha256 of the warning text the page showed): the operation checks that the model is a model id of the gate file, that the mode is the agent's own and one of the consent modes, that the hash is the current and accepted configuration, that the warning's hash is the sha256 of `CONSENT_WARNING`, and that the page's policy lists the group `consent` (not `set-mode`: granting a mode never grants consent); it then writes that agent's model and consent, accepts that one change (recorded `person:page`) and nothing else. A messaging channel, the MCP mode and the conversation have neither operation. The warning is true whatever the evidence is: consent matters only for the skills of the agent that are not proven on the model, and the others are released by the mode as for any proven model. Changing the model or the mode asks again, except a move DOWN the order of the modes, which is never withheld: from `autonomous-with-policy` to `autonomous` a valid consent follows to the lower mode, and to any other mode it is dropped, widening nothing, so code accepts that hash at once; going back to `auto` or the default drops the consent likewise.
- **The key.** The runtime's own key for a model is the secret `WB_RUNTIME_KEY_<the variable its credential travels under>` (the registry `runtime/secrets.json` lists today's two; the one for `OPENROUTER_API_KEY` is stored under the username `runtime-floor`, as the single key of earlier releases was), which wins when stored; else the lab's key for the model (`models[<id>].pass_env` of the gate file). The routing names the key by its source, never its value. A model with no key is not a candidate of `auto`; a named model with no key does not start, and says why.

## The sources

Package P4 of the delegation platform plan (decisions D9, D10, D11, D13, D16, D29, D30). The page `docs/architecture/platform/sources.md` is the map; this is the contract.

- **The folder.** `sources_dir` in `runtime.json` is mandatory, absolute, with no default, accepted only in the terminal like every key of the file. It is dedicated to the runtime (empty, or marked with `.openhora-sources` when the configuration is accepted: `accept-config` creates a missing folder with mode 0700 and marks an empty one; a folder with anything else in it, or with a mode other than 0700, is refused and nothing is accepted; the marker names the project it was made for by the project folder's identity, its device and inode (not how a path spells it: a case-insensitive disk keeps the case a person typed), with its path for the message; a folder marked for another project, or for none, is refused with the two identities in the message and a word to name a separate folder for each project. The same project moved to another disk, or on a disk mounted again, is taken over only by `accept-config --adopt-sources` in the terminal, the person's own act; the page, the conversation and `set-mode` cannot), disjoint from `data_dir`, `store_db`, the checkout and the local evidence folder, and it may sit inside the project (the work copy excludes it, limit L26), but not at or under the project's `docs/` or `.workbench-local/`: the documents mirror, the file drop and the copies of the sources a task receives are written there by path. The configuration is refused at load when the path is the filesystem root, the home folder, a link (written with a closing slash or `/.` too; a link higher up, like `/tmp` on macOS, is not a reason), a folder that contains the project, `docs/` or `.workbench-local/` of the project or a folder inside either, or not disjoint from the others; the rest is checked at acceptance and before every use. A brake is accepted whatever the state of the folder: `set-mode` to a lower mode (code accepts the new hash, `code:narrowing`) does not look at the folder, which every use of it checks again; accepting the configuration as a person does. Inside the folder, a name that is not a regular file where a file is expected (a pipe, a device, a link) is refused before it is opened, so a read never waits on it. The numbered limits are L21 to L27 below.
- **A version.** `<sources_dir>/<source id>/<version>/original.<ext>`, `converted.md` or `converted.csv`, and `manifest.json` (origin, the date from the clock, sha256 and size of the original and of the converted text, the converter and its tools, `hidden_text` null until P8, warnings, the origin's own version for a Notion page). Written whole in `.incoming/` and moved into place with one rename; every file mode 0400; a refresh is the next number after the newest RECORDED version (the only number the store accepts) and no file is written again. A numbered folder that holds no record (a crash between the rename and the row, a removal that failed, a stray folder) is not counted: the next import moves it into `.incoming/` as `<token>-orphan` through the folder's descriptor, without following a link, where the staging sweep removes it later, and then puts the new version there. The same holds for numbered folders with no row after the store is restored from an older backup: they are moved to `.incoming` and removed by the sweep after 24 hours. The registry, the versions' rows and the catalog are in the store (migration 11: tables `sources`, `source_versions`, `task_sources`, and `tasks.sources`); none is ever deleted or changed but a source's description.
- **An import.** The file is streamed in by the generic intake (`runtime/intake.py`: the length is bounded before a byte is read, the type is read from the bytes with the sniffers `runtime/sources.py` adds, a file of any other type is refused before it exists); the converter of the class `converter:document` writes `converted.md` or `converted.csv` and `info.json` into a folder of the staging area, `<data_dir>/sources-staging/<token>/` (the runtime's own, outside the sources folder, made through the descriptor of each parent with `O_NOFOLLOW`, this user's, mode 0700; a link at its name is refused; the provider's host side: `providers/converter/container.py`; a container with no network, a read-only root, every capability dropped, a memory-backed `/scratch` with no exec, the input mounted read-only; its result is a stream of frames on standard output, capped by the host, never a folder it left); `collect_outputs` reads the folder back (limit L24); the converted text must be UTF-8 text, within the cap, and passes the credential scan of limit L14 (a key hidden in a PDF's compressed text is found there); only then are the version moved into place and its rows written. A refusal leaves nothing. Caps: an original is at most 25 MiB, the converted text at most 1 MiB, and a text over 256 KiB is kept with a warning.
- **A Notion page.** `source-import --url <address or id>` extracts the 32-character id (`sources.notion_page_id`) and calls the documents provider's `read --as-source`, which uses the separate read-only secret `NOTION_SOURCES_TOKEN` and never `NOTION_TOKEN`; the Markdown it returns is a version like any other (no container: it is already text), with the page's `last_edited_time` in the manifest; a refresh that reads the same text makes no new version.
- **A Google Drive file.** `source-drive` opens the Picker on a one-shot page on 127.0.0.1 and makes each FILE the person picks a source of type `drive` (P7 stored it as a `file` row whose origin is `drive:<file id>`; migration 14 made it the type `drive`, see "Routines" below); `source-drive --file-id <id> --source <id>` imports one file already picked. The documents provider `gdrive` (scope `drive.file`, secret `GDRIVE_SOURCES_TOKEN`, GET requests only) writes the exported or downloaded bytes into the runtime's staging area: a Google Doc as Markdown (a version with the converter `markdown`, no container), a Sheet as `.xlsx`, Slides as PDF, another file as it is; a folder, a drawing and a file in the trash are refused. They enter through the same intake, converter, credential scan and rows, and the manifest carries the file's name and the provider's version as `origin_version`. `source-refresh` asks the provider's `stat` for that version first and reads nothing when it matches. The provider's process has the minimum environment and its one secret, a run's environment holds none, and no folder is ever a source.
- **A OneDrive file (O2).** There is no picker: `source-onedrive --search <text>` (or `--folder <path>`) lists up to 25 candidates from the person's own OneDrive and registers nothing, and `source-onedrive --item <drive id>/<item id>` (repeatable) makes each chosen FILE a source of type `onedrive` (the type column admits it since migration 19; the origin is `<drive id>/<item id>`) and imports its first version. The documents provider `onedrive` (delegated Microsoft Graph scope `Files.Read` and `offline_access`, secret `ONEDRIVE_SOURCES_TOKEN`, GET requests only) writes the downloaded bytes into the runtime's staging area; they enter through the same intake, converter, credential scan and rows, and the manifest carries the file's name, the provider's version (the item's cTag) as `origin_version` and the eTag. `source-refresh` asks the provider's `stat` for the cTag first and reads nothing when it matches (a share or a rename moves the eTag and not the cTag). A folder, a OneNote notebook, an item shared from another drive, a flagged or deleted item and a legacy .doc, .xls or .ppt are refused with the provider's reason. **`Files.Read` reads every file of the person's own OneDrive and writes none** (Microsoft has no scope per chosen file): the narrowing to the chosen files is the runtime's, and the provider's process has the minimum environment and its one secret, which a run's environment, a manifest, the catalog and a message never hold. Only the terminal registers a OneDrive source (`source-onedrive`; `source-add --type onedrive` is refused from the page and every other channel); `source-refresh` of a registered one may come from the page.
- **Mail and a calendar (RT-1).** A window of the person's mailbox or of one calendar is a source of type `mail` or `calendar` (the type column admits them since migration 21), read as a dated SNAPSHOT and never live. **Only the terminal registers one** (`source-mail`, `source-calendar` and `source-add --type mail|calendar` are refused on the page, the MCP mode, a messaging app and the conversation, as `source-onedrive` is; the two rows list the terminal alone and are in no group); a registered one is read again by `source-refresh`, also from the page, and by the `ingest` step. **`/ask` reads one only when the question names it with `@<id>`**, from the terminal, the conversation's own shell or the page; a messaging app is refused (decision of 2026-10-18, `docs/decisions.md`). `source-mail --source <id> --origin gmail:box=inbox;days=7;max=50` (or `outlook:box=unread;days=3;max=20`; the box is one of the fixed ones, Gmail `inbox`, `important`, `starred` and `unread`, Outlook `inbox` and `unread`; 1 to 31 days back, 1 to 100 messages) and `source-calendar --source <id> --origin google:calendar=primary;past=0;ahead=14` (or `outlook:calendar=default;past=1;ahead=7`; one calendar, 0 to 7 days before today and 0 to 31 after) register the source and read its first snapshot; a registered source is read again by the same command with no origin (in the terminal), by `source-refresh` and by the `ingest` step of a routine. The runtime calls the provider (`reader:email` `gmail` or `outlook`, `integration:calendar` `google` or `outlook`, the implementation named in the origin, never "auto") with the minimum environment and its own secret, builds the text (`runtime/snapshot_text.py`) and imports it as the next version with the label `import` or `refresh`: the sender's own words only (the quoted history cut by the provider), every link replaced by `<link: host>` and every `mailto:` address by `<address>`, control and direction characters removed, a body cut at 4,000 characters and indented, attachments listed by name, declared type and size and never opened, no event description and no attendee, and a heading with a number and a time or a date and nothing a third party wrote. **Each message (each event) is scanned for credentials on its own; one that holds a credential shape is left out and counted by its date and its sender's domain, never redacted**, and the whole text then passes `import_version`'s own scan as a second guard. The window comes from the host's clock and zone and is written in the manifest (`window_start`, `window_end`, `zone`, `read_at`). The listing identifies the reading: `origin_version` is the sha256 of the sorted (id, change key) pairs, the zone and the local date, so the same list on the same day makes no version and a changed one, or the same one on another day, makes one. A snapshot older than 26 hours is never handed to a task that starts on its own (L53) and the providers are called by the source operations alone (L52). The scope of each reader reads the whole mailbox or every calendar (`gmail.readonly`, `Mail.Read`, the calendar scopes) and changes none; the narrowing to a box, a window and one calendar is the runtime's. Messages, titles and places are external content: data, never an instruction. **The index of a snapshot (SNAP-IDX).** Beside the text, the manifest of each mail or calendar version keeps `index`: one entry for each item the text lists, in the order the text lists it, numbered from 1, so that a request to change a message or an event names it by `<source id>#<n>` and code finds it by the index, never by the words of the text (`runtime/item_refs.py`). A mail text prints the numbers (`## 3. ...`) and so does a calendar text (`3. 09:00-10:00 · ...`), in the order of `snapshot_text.calendar_order` (day by day, all-day first, then by start), so the number a person reads is the `n` of the entry. A mail entry is `{n, id, thread_id, message_id, from_address, reply_to_address, subject, change_key}` (the provider's ids, the Message-ID header, the sender's plain address and a Reply-To that differs from it, both printed by the reader as an address alone, the Gmail reader only when the header holds exactly one mailbox and the Outlook reader from Graph's address field, never picked out of a name; the subject with its links replaced, control and direction characters removed, cut at 120 characters, so it may end in "…"; Graph's change key); an event entry is `{n, id, etag, calendar, organizer_self, attendees_other, recurring}` (`attendees_other` is null when the reader did not print it, which is today). Every value is a closed shape or null (a null id keeps its entry, so that the numbers do not move); there is no body, no sender's name and no token; a message or an event left out of the text for a credential has no entry and takes no number; an entry is scanned once more as a whole. The index is in the manifest, which no run receives (L26, L52), and **its sha256 is the third line of the text** (`Index digest (sha256): ...`), which the store's hash of the text (L25) binds: an index edited in the manifest no longer matches its version and `item_refs` refuses it. A reference to a snapshot older than 26 hours, or to a version made before the index existed, is refused with "refresh <id> first".
- **The catalog.** Built by code at import from the converted text alone (limit L27): name, type, size, date, sheets and columns with up to three sample values each (cut at 40 characters), the first eight headings, the person's description and warnings. It is third-party text.
- **The planner.** When the project has a source with a version, the router's prompt carries the catalog (`ops_sources.router_block`) after the task's text, with the sentence that it is data. The router skill (`core-orchestrator` 3.0.0) names the sources of each skill on a `Sources:` line (`runtime/router.py`: `Sources: <skill>: <id>, <id>; <skill>: <id>`, a bare list for every task, `none`); a reply with no such line chooses none. `check_route` refuses an id the project does not hold; `plan.combine` puts the ids in the plan's tasks (so `plan_hash` covers them) and refuses a skill that is not in the plan; an `@<source id>` in the request forces that source into every task of the delivery. The plan card lists each task's sources and highlights a task that uses the web and receives sources.
- **A task.** When a task starts, the newest version of each source id its plan chose is hashed against the record and copied (converted text only) to `.workbench-local/sources/<source id>/` of the run's copy; the run's prompt lists the copies as data; the rows `task_sources` record the id, version and sha256. A version changed outside the runtime is not handed over (the task fails before any model is called, and says to run `source-refresh`); the newest version made by the person (`changed-by-person`) is given only to a task the person starts: `run-next`, which is a terminal verb (the page cannot start a task). A start by the dispatcher and a start with `run-task` (also a terminal verb, but the dispatcher's own: it starts the task checked) are unattended and refuse that version, saying to start the task with `run-next` or to import a new file for the source (a new file is a new version made by the runtime; an import of the same bytes changes nothing). A copy that fails the credential scan again, or cannot be read, stops the task with the reason, and the rows `task_sources` are written only for the copies the run received (after its copy is built).
- **A question.** `ask` ("The answer from sources") hands the converted text of the sources code selected to one run of the answer skill and nothing else of the folder: the same copies, the same checks, and the version the person changed by hand only to an ask typed in the terminal or the conversation.
- **The channels.** `source-add`, `source-import`, `source-refresh` and `source-describe` list the terminal and the page (`source-add` registers a OneDrive, a mail or a calendar source from the terminal alone); `sources` (the catalog) lists those and the MCP mode and the messaging channel, and the five are the group `sources` of the channel policy, which is not in the default policy of messaging; `ask` is the group `ask`, in no default either. No ingest is a task before package P5.
- **Files from messaging apps (ATT-1).** A file a listed sender sends through a messaging app is held, checked and shown on a card; it becomes a source only when a person adds it (the card's button, `/add <n>`, the page or the terminal) or when the sender's profile has the grant `add-sources-direct`. A channel whose policy lacks the group `add-sources` takes no file: nothing is downloaded (the default policy of messaging lacks it; the MCP mode has no row). **The operations** are `attachments` (the held list, the latest decisions, the day's count and the limits; terminal, chat, page, messaging), `attachment-hold` (the bridge's call after a fetch; messaging alone), `attachment-add` and `attachment-discard` (terminal, page, messaging; never the conversation: from a phone a file is decided only by the answer to its card), all four in the group `add-sources`. **On a messaging app a sender sees and decides only the files THEY sent**: `attachments` returns the acting sender's own rows, and `attachment-add` and `attachment-discard` answer "No file n waits for you." for a file whose platform and sender are not the acting sender's (`ops_attachments._own`); the terminal and the page, which are the person's own, see every file; `add-sources-direct` is a grant of the messaging channel (`operations.GRANTS`, accepted only with `accept-config --widen`) that holds `attachment-hold` with the kind `direct`: the operation checks the grant against the sender's profile itself (`operations.granted`), so a bridge that asks for a direct add for a sender without it is refused. **It makes new sources and never a new version of one that exists**: a file whose caption names a registered source (even one with no version yet) is held with a card instead (`direct_declined`). **The limits** are constants in `runtime/attachments.py`, fixed in code: a file at most `FILE_CAP` (the intake's 25 MiB, or the provider's own `max_attachment_bytes` when lower), 20 files and 100 MiB in a UTC day for the project with every platform together (a file counts when its bytes were fetched, a refused one included), 10 files held at once, 5 files taken from one message, 7 days held. A voice note, a video, an audio file, a sticker and an image of a type other than PNG, JPEG, WebP, GIF or HEIC are refused before download (no OCR or transcription yet). **Images are save-only holds (ATT-3).** A photo (the platform's word; always a JPEG, named `photo-<date>-<time>.jpg` when it has no name) and an image file of those five types are held like any file, but never converted, never a source, never shown to a model: the bytes must be the image the declared type says (`mismatch` otherwise; a generic declared type goes by the bytes), the hold has no source id (`target` and `label` are null, `save_only` is true), the extension of the name is the one the bytes say, `attachment-add` answers "Images are not added to the project; I can save them to Drive or OneDrive." and leaves the file held, and the grant `add-sources-direct` never adds one. The size, day and held limits above apply as to any file. **One card for each file.** The card says "I received <name> (<size>, <type>). What should I do?" with a button for what THAT sender's profile permits: a save to each account the project lists with folders (`file-save`; the button opens the W4a card of the exact content, which is the approval), **Add to project** (a type that can be a source, `attachment-add`) and **Discard**; a file still comes in only through `add-sources` (the hold, the add and the discard are one group), so the add button is hidden only for an image. A caption only preselects, by closed word lists of the language tables: one service named, or a save word with one account listed, opens the save card at once and sends no other card for the file; anything else is the one card. A later line about the sender's last held file (`save to drive`, `discard`, `add to the project`) acts on it only while the file is less than an hour old and its card is in the chat (any other time it is an ordinary line); with several files their cards come back, with none the line is answered "Send the file and say where to save it." A save is the W4a save: the writers take the images' media types for a held file only. **Every fetch counts against the day**, also one that ended with no file: the bridge reports it with `attachment-hold --failed gone|fetch|size --fetched-bytes <n>` (the bytes it was allowed or wrote), and the hold records a refused row for a duplicate, a page upload in progress, a declared medium or size and any rule that refused the file after its bytes arrived; the row keeps the cleaned name, the reference as a hash and no file. The intake is bound by the least of the 25 MiB, the provider's cap and the day's bytes left (`max_bytes`), and the provider's file must have no other name (`st_nlink` 1). **The hold.** The provider writes the file in `<data_dir>/attachments/incoming/<token>/content`; `attachment-hold` streams it through `intake.stream_in` (length bounded, type read from the bytes by the sources sniffers, hash while it passes, one upload at a time with the page's) and keeps it as `held/<id>/original` (mode 0400), records it in the table `attachments` (migration 18: the sender's id, the platform's reference only as a hash, the cleaned name, the declared and the sniffed type, the file's sha256, the source it would become and whether that is an `import` or a `refresh`, the status `held`, `added`, `refused`, `discarded` or `expired`) and removes the incoming folder whatever happens. A declared specific type of the converter's list that the bytes contradict is refused (`mismatch`); a generic or absent one goes by the bytes. The source id is the exact `@<id>` the caption named (an existing `file` source: a new version labelled `refresh`; a free id: a new source; a `notion` or `drive` source: refused), else made from the name (`sources.source_id_from_name`) so that it names no source and no other held file; any other caption text is not read. **The add** reads the held bytes back, hashes them against the row, looks at the target again (a source that changed since refuses the add and leaves the file held) and calls `ops_sources.import_bytes` with the provenance `{channel: messaging, platform, profile, attachment}`: the converter container, the credential scan of the converted text, the hidden-text flag and the size warning of any import, then the immutable version, whose `manifest.json`, row and catalog line (`sent on telegram, profile staff`) carry that provenance and never the sender.
