A plan approved from `messaging` carries its hash (code in `ops_waits.only_on_plans`, called from `ops.approve`: "a plan is approved from a messaging app only with its hash"); an acceptance has none, so the bridge binds it to what the person saw: before it approves, it reads the decision again and compares the sha256 of its text with the hash recorded for the card, and when they differ it approves nothing, replies "This decision changed since you saw it." and sends the new card. A command typed into `say` from `messaging` is done only when the channel's row lists it and the policy permits it, as the same channel (`ops_say` checks it under `messaging`, not `chat`, so these rules apply); the bridge itself handles `/approve`, `/reject`, `/release`, `/answer`, `/pending`, `/project` and `/help`, binding approve to the ledger's card. **Each listed sender has the profile the configuration gives them** (POL-2, "Policies per person" below, limit L46): the bridge builds the sender's key (`<platform>:<id>`, from the update the provider authenticated; a private chat's `chat` is that sender's), asks the policy for that sender at every tap and line, draws each card for its sender, refuses a card whose ledger `chat` is not the acting sender's, passes the sender to the operation (which asks again and records the actor), and sends a profile with `notices: actionable` a card only for a decision it can act on or answer.

**The channel policy** (decisions M-8, M-12, M-13, M-42). The `channels` column of the table is the ceiling of a channel; a project narrows it with named groups in `docs/workbench/runtime.json` (`"channels": {"page": [...], "mcp": [...], "messaging": [...]}`, each a list of groups or the name of a profile, below), and widens it only by a grant (below). A change takes effect after `accept-config` in the terminal, like every key of the file, and a file that gives a channel or a listed sender any group the accepted file did not effectively give it is accepted only with `--widen` and the hash of the widening list. It holds for the three channels that take a policy: `page` (the local service), `mcp` (the MCP mode) and `messaging` (the bridge). The terminal is never narrowed, and neither is `chat` (the conversation's own shell: its input is local access, equal to the terminal); `channels.terminal` and `channels.chat` are configuration errors. `runtime/project_config.py` (`_channels`) checks the file: an unknown group, a group none of whose operations the channel's ceiling lists, `approve-effects` anywhere but `page`, an effect group or `approve-networked-plans` on the MCP mode, `approve-networked-plans` anywhere but `messaging` or without `approve-plans`, and a `page` or `mcp` list without `read` are errors that name the reason; an unknown group is refused with the list of the known ones.

**The groups** are a closed list, a pure literal in `runtime/operations.py` (`GROUPS`), and every row of the table that the page, the MCP mode or messaging may call is in exactly one group, `cancel` excepted and `read-limited` (a subset of `read`) beside it (a test walks the table; `GROUP_TEXT` says in one line what each group gives, and `IMPLIED` which group holds another; `approve` is a group for each kind of decision: plans, acceptances, the umbrella `approve-effects`, one `approve-effect-<word>` for each effect word an approval path opens, and `approve-networked-plans`). `read`: `status`, `progress`, `pending`, `task`, `agents`, `costs`, `skills`, `artifacts`, `artifact`, `flows`, `conversation`, `connections`, `version`, `config`, `commands`, `artifact-raw`. `talk`: `say`, which also creates and routes the requests a line makes and answers the planning agent's own open question in the conversation. `answer`: `answer`. `release`: `release`. `request`: `request` and `route`. `approve-plans`, `approve-acceptances` and `approve-effects`: `approve` of a decision of that kind (`approve-effects`, the umbrella that covers every effect word, is valid on the page only). `approve-effect-create`, `approve-effect-write`, `approve-effect-send` and `approve-effect-publish`: `approve` of an effect of that word (a pull request, a write to the documents platform, an e-mail, a post); valid on the page, where they narrow the umbrella, and on messaging, where they are grants. `approve-networked-plans`: `approve` of a plan that holds a step of a networked kind (limit L36), messaging only, on top of `approve-plans`. `go-ahead`: the operation `go-ahead` and a non-empty `go_ahead` inside `approve` (which needs the approve group of the plan as well). `read-limited` (POL-2): `read` without `costs`, `agents`, `connections` and `config`, and without `task` and `progress`, which carry a run's cost (valid on messaging only; a profile lists `read` or `read-limited`, not both). `reject-cancel`: `reject` and `cancel`. `brakes` (R18): `cancel` and `stop-task`, turned on for messaging by listing it (in no default); `cancel` is in both groups, the one operation that is, so that a policy written with `reject-cancel` stays valid. `retry`: `retry`. `set-mode`: `set-mode` and `set-model`. `consent`: `give-consent`, the page's consent for an autonomous agent (D38), a group of its own: a project that granted `set-mode` to the page before it existed does not thereby grant consent, and the page can consent only when its policy lists `consent` (a page with no `channels.page` entry keeps its whole ceiling, so it may consent; a policy that lists `set-mode` and not `consent` cannot). `dispatch`: `dispatch`. `documents`: `sync` and `hand-over`. `verdict`: `verdict`. `sources`: `source-add`, `source-import`, `source-refresh`, `source-describe` (the terminal and the page) and `sources`, the catalog (also the MCP mode and messaging), turned on by a project's policy and in no default (P4). `ask`: `ask`, one question answered from the full text of the project's sources (the terminal, the conversation, the page and messaging, never the MCP mode), in no default: a messaging sender gets it only from a profile or channel entry accepted with `--widen` (AQ-1, "The answer from sources"). `flow-write`: `flow-save` and `stage-file` (the page; it is not `flows`, the read operation of the group `read`, so that listing `flows` in a policy is refused and never grants a write). `routine-brakes`: `routine-pause` and `routine-cancel` (in the default policy of messaging). `routine-edit`: `routine-create` and `routine-change`. `routine-approve`: `routine-approve` and `routine-resume` (the terminal and the page). `instructions-write`: `instructions-set` and `instructions-add` (the page; it was `instructions`, renamed with no alias because that is the read operation of the group `read`). `confirm-reads-off` (BL2a, 2026-10-24; the policy "confirm-reads" is on by default for every messaging sender and this group turns it off): `say`, as a kind-only group of messaging in no default (it never grants `say`), so that a profile that lists its groups keeps the confirmation on; listing it is a widening shown by `accept-config` and typed with `--widen`; it turns off the question "Shall I go ahead?" before a read asked in words and never before research, `ask` or an act (L65). (`reject-cancel-retry`, the first spelling, was split into `reject-cancel` and `retry`; it was in no real configuration.) A group is valid on a channel when at least one of its operations is in that channel's ceiling; it then covers the ceiling's share of the group (`read` on `mcp` is the reads the MCP mode has).

**Defaults.** A `page` or `mcp` entry that is absent keeps the whole ceiling, so a project that writes no `channels` behaves as before; when listed, it must include `read`. A `messaging` entry that is absent is the closed default `read`, `talk`, `answer`, `routine-brakes`; approving, releasing, rejecting, cancelling and retrying from a messaging app are turned on explicitly. No default holds a grant.

**The grants** (POL-1, decision M-40: a channel restriction is a policy, whose default is today's rule and which the person may widen). "Grant" means a group of `operations.GRANTS` and nothing else: the groups that go beyond a channel's ceiling. The groups inside the ceiling (`approve-plans`, `release`, `reject-cancel` on messaging, `retry`, `brakes`, `routine-edit`, ...) are not grants, but listing one is a widening all the same (POL-2, below): `accept-config` takes the hash of the widening list for any group a place did not effectively have, because the default is the restrictive rule and a file edited by someone else must not add a power in an acceptance made without reading. `operations.GRANTS` is the one list of widenings a configuration can ask for: for `messaging`, `approve-effect-create`, `approve-effect-write`, `approve-effect-send`, `approve-effect-publish` and `approve-networked-plans`; for `mcp`, `approve-plans`, `approve-acceptances`, `reject-cancel` and `retry`. `GRANT_TEXT` says in one sentence what each gives away; `NEVER_GRANTED` (`chat`, `terminal`) and a test hold that no effect group, no networked-plan group and no group of consent, `set-mode` or routine approval is ever a grant of the MCP mode. A grant is reached only from the project's own entry for the channel (`operations.granted`; `effective_groups` is the one seam every check reads), never through `DEFAULT_POLICY` or "no entry keeps the ceiling". `policy_allows` asks, in this order: a plan with a networked step (`plan:networked`: the page asks as for a plan, any other channel needs the grant), an effect (`effect:<word>`) on a channel outside `EFFECT_CHANNELS` (only a grant of that word; `effect` with no word is false, and the umbrella never counts there), a row that does not list the channel (only a grant that covers the operation: the MCP mode's `approve`, `reject`, `cancel`, `retry`), and the rest as before. **What stays code**, whatever the configuration lists: the approval of the exact hash (`ops._approve_effect`), the whole text on the card (`messaging_cards.build` and the ledger's `whole`), the effect's own `on_cards` (an e-mail with an attachment, a post with an image), a line typed into any channel never approving an effect, `accept-config` and its `--widen` only in the terminal, the sender allowlist, the brakes, and the terminal never narrowed. **Accepting a widening:** `config` shows `grants`, `widening` (every group a place has in the file and did not effectively have in the one accepted before: `[{"place", "channel", "group", "means"}]`, the place being a channel or `<platform>:<sender id>`, `means` the text of `GRANT_TEXT` or `GROUP_TEXT`) and `widening_sha256`, the sha256 of the widening list written one `<place>:<group>` line each with a newline after it (messaging, then the MCP mode, then the page, then the senders by key); `accept-config --sha256 <the file> --widen <that hash>` accepts a file that widens, any other accepts nothing and says what it would give away; what the accepted file effectively gives is recorded in the store's cursor `config:effective` (`policy_widening.py`, written by `ops_config._record_acceptance`, so `set-mode --accept` and the page's consent record the same and widen nothing). A project accepted before the record existed has POL-1's cursor `config:grants` or nothing, and cannot say what its accepted file gave: the baseline is "unknown" (only those grants were confirmed), `config` lists every group of every channel, an absent entry being its default or its ceiling, and every listed sender, and the person runs `accept-config --widen <the hash config shows>` once (the file stays in force meanwhile). Only `accept-config` writes the record; a mode change by code or `set-mode --accept` does not end the unknown state. A sender the accepted file did not list is compared with nothing, with a profile or without: adding a person lists their groups once. The configuration also warns (never refuses) that a messaging effect grant does nothing while `messaging.notices` is `count`, and that a profile is assigned to no one.

**Policies per person** (POL-2, decision M-42). A policy is a named set of groups, a *profile* (`runtime.json` `policies`: a list of groups, or `{"groups": [...], "notices": "all" | "actionable"}`); a channel has a default (`channels.<channel>` is a list or a profile name) and, on a messaging app, each entry of `messaging.senders.<platform>` is a plain id or `{"id", "policy"}`, whose profile replaces the channel's groups for that sender. `operations.effective_groups(channel, cfg, sender)` is the one resolution: the sender's profile, else the channel's entry, else `DEFAULT_POLICY`, else the whole ceiling, and a grant counts only from an explicit entry or profile; `sender` is `operations.sender_key(platform, id)`, read for messaging only. `project_config` refuses an unknown profile, a profile that lists an unknown group or both `read` and `read-limited`, a profile assigned to a channel that does not take one of its groups (naming the profile and the channel), a sender listed twice on a platform, an id that is not text without a space of at most 64 characters, an entry object with keys other than `id` and `policy`, a profile named `default`, more than 100 senders in all, and a profile for `chat` or `terminal`. **An act from a messaging app is checked against the profile of the sender who acted, read from the accepted configuration when they act, and, for a tap and a decision typed by id, only in the chat its card was sent to (L46; `cancel`, `stop-task` and `retry` are by id and need the group):** the bridge asks `permitted(op, "messaging", policy, kind, sender)` at each tap and line and passes the sender to the operation (`ops.approve`, `reject`, `release`, `retry`, `answer`, `cancel`, `stop-task`, `say`), which asks again for that sender (`ops_actor.guard`) and records the actor with the decision. A call from a messaging app that names no sender is refused (`ops_actor.require`; the bridge raises before it asks the policy), and a command typed into a line is not done from a messaging app by an operation that cannot be told the sender. The record is the `actor` column of `pending_decisions` and `approvals` (store migration 17; the approvals trigger refuses to change it): `terminal`, `page`, `mcp`, `chat` or `messaging:<platform>:<profile>:<sender id>`; it is in the store alone, under the project's data folder and outside git. The project's versioned files and the board get the platform and the profile name only: the state file's generated approval row says "approved on telegram, profile owner", a cancel's label "user from messaging, profile owner", and what a shell reads (`pending <id>`, `task`, `progress`) shows `acted_by` and no id. A retry has no row that could carry an actor, so it is checked for the sender and recorded nowhere. The id of an entry that was removed stays on the old rows. A profile with `notices: actionable` is sent a card only for a decision its card can act on or answer; a sender whose profile permits no read is sent none. The page and the MCP mode have no person identity (the terminal is whoever uses the machine, the page whoever holds the token, MCP the client process): per-person policies there wait for the sign-in of the hosting project, and `channels.page: "owner"` already works.

**The brakes.** `cancel`, `stop-task`, and `set-mode` to a lower mode (`operations.BRAKES`), are permitted on the page whatever its policy, `--lan` included, so a person who has lost trust can always stop work from the page; a page policy may omit them without error. They do not leak to the MCP mode, which has neither; on `messaging` they are closed by default like every other write (the default policy holds `routine-brakes` and not `brakes`: every channel is restrictive by default and the person widens it), and `channels.messaging` listing `brakes` (or `reject-cancel`, for `cancel` alone) turns them on. A move *up* of `set-mode` from the page is refused with the sentence of the policy when the page's policy lacks `set-mode`; the direction is known only after the mode is read, so `ops_config.set_mode` checks it (before its lock and any write), and `permitted` answers true for the operation.

**The helper and where it runs.** `operations.permitted(op_name, channel, policy, kind=None, sender=None)` is pure; `operations.refusal(op_name, channel, policy, kind=None, go_ahead=False, sender=None)` returns the sentence "/<operation> is not enabled for this channel; the project's configuration (channels.<channel>) turns it on", or None, and the caller raises `OpsError` with code 1. It runs in code, in each shell and in the operations layer, never in a prompt: `service.py` for every route of the page, after the query is read and before the bytes of an upload are written and before a job starts (so a refused route takes no model slot and answers `409` `refused`); `mcp.py` at each `tools/call`, after the arguments are read and before a job starts; `ops_say._say_command` for a command typed into `say`, under the channel that carries the line (`page`, `mcp` or `messaging`), so `/approve <acceptance>` typed on the page under a policy that lacks `approve-acceptances` is refused; `ops_waits.only_on_plans` (called by `ops.approve`) for plans, acceptances and effects, and for a non-empty `go_ahead`; and `ops_config.set_mode` for the direction. `ops.approve` asks an effect by its word (`ops_grants.effect_facts` reads the effect document by the decision's own hash; `ops.pending <id>` adds `effect_word` and `effect_on_cards`), and `ops_grants.effect_refusal` refuses it to a channel outside `EFFECT_CHANNELS` unless the policy grants the word and the whole effect fits on a card. A caller first asks `ops.config` (it never refuses): a configuration that is not accepted is left to the operation, which answers `412` as it always did, otherwise the `channels` of that answer are the policy. A read returns before that, so the per-second polls ask nothing more. `permitted` is never the only guard of an effect: an effect is approved only from `EFFECT_CHANNELS` with its hash (`ops.approve`), and **a line typed into any channel never approves an effect**: `_say_command` reads the decision's kind first and refuses an effect, whatever the policy, with the sentence that names the terminal and the command.

**The MCP mode.** `tools/list` shows the ceiling's tools whatever the policy, plus the grant-only tools (`approve`, `reject`, `cancel`, `retry`) when a project served has accepted a grant that covers them; the check that enforces a narrower `channels.mcp` is made at each `tools/call`, per project, with the policy that project accepted, so two projects served by one process may differ. A plan is approved from here only with its hash (`sha256`). A refused call is a tool result with `isError: true` and the sentence. `say` is the group `talk`; `request` and `route` are the group `request`. The MCP mode keeps its ceiling unless the person narrows it.

**The queue.** Only the page queues a line or a route in the store while a run holds the project (a messaging app keeps one waiting line per chat in the bridge's memory instead: "Waiting lines" below). Each queue entry stores the group that created it (`group`: `talk` for a line and for a request a line made, `request` for a route the page asked for); `route_queued` re-checks that group under `page`, with the policy as it is at drain time (after any `accept-config`), before it answers the line or routes the request, and acts as `DRAIN` otherwise so that the order stays first in, first out. A refused entry gets a stored reply with the sentence (for a line, in the conversation; for a route, as a note on the request) and leaves the queue. An entry queued before `group` was stored is a line (`talk`) or a route (`request`), checked under `page`, never as `queue`.

**What the page reads.** The service's own `GET /projects` adds, per project, `"permissions"`: `{"channel": "page", "allowed": [...]}`, or `null` when the configuration is not accepted (nothing is permitted yet). `allowed` is in the table's order and holds the plain name of every operation the page may call, except `approve` and `set-mode`, which are pairs: `approve:plan`, `approve:acceptance`, `approve:effect`, `approve:effect:<word>` for each word the policy covers, `approve:go-ahead` and `set-mode:down`, `set-mode:up` (and, when the project grants something, `"grants"`: `[{"channel", "group", "text"}]`); `cancel` and `set-mode:down` are always there on the page. It is built in `service.py` (`operations.permissions`), not in `shell_kit`, which the MCP mode shares: the MCP `projects` tool keeps the kit's shape. The page's `commands` answer (the conversation's commands as rows) leaves out the commands the page's policy refuses; `/new` follows `talk` and `/help` stays. The page reads that list (`interface/js/permissions.js`, `can(project, op, kind)`) and draws only the controls it allows, with one line saying what is turned off; `cancel` and a move down of an agent's mode are exempt as brakes, a project entry without the key allows everything, and the service's `409` stays the authority for a write the page still sends. The policy governs a person's requests, not the dispatch loop that the service runs on its own timer, which keeps calling `dispatch` whatever the page's policy says.

**A phone never reads a command or a path** (package LIVE-2, limit L62). A messaging reply, card or file carries no shell command of this platform (`uv run ...`, `python3 .../cli.py ...`, `--project`, `--sha256`...) and no path of this machine, and no hash that only the terminal's command needs: the terminal, the page and the conversation keep the exact command because there it can be run. What the phone can do it does (a write asked in words opens its card); what is kept off it by design (the acceptance of a configuration, accounts and their authorization, secrets, permissions and policies, sources added from the terminal, routines created, the standing instructions, the typed-only configuration of class G, a reminder to bring back that the router chose, the approval of an effect whose card is not whole) is answered with ONE plain sentence in the project's language that says what is done and where, and the page link (`messaging.page_url`; without it "on the platform's page"): the phrase keys of the family `PHONE_` (`phrases.phone`, which fills `{where}`), chosen per operation family by `ops_natural.PHONE_FAMILY`. An effect card says "Approve it on the platform's page." and, when `page_url` is set, carries the link as a button or in the footer; it carries no approve command, and its "Approving applies exactly the text above." names no hash (the button still binds to the hash the ledger recorded, and a card that changed is refused: unchanged). The rule is about what the PLATFORM writes, never about what is quoted from the person's data: a card shows its body exactly (minus the private-line rules and the platform's own lines for a terminal: the card's `*_APPROVE` line, the hash lines `CARD_MAIL_HASH` / `CARD_FILE_HASH`, the short hash of the save line, the stored body's approve how-to line, and the publisher's ledger path said as "a file on this machine"), so a calendar title, a mail subject or text, a file's text or a plan's step that looks like a command or a path is the person's, is shown whole and the card's approvability is what it was. The net under the sentences is `runtime/messaging_guard.py`, applied by `Bridge.deliver(guard_text=True)` / `Bridge.reply(guard=True)` to the one platform text that is not built from a phrase key, the error text of the operations layer: it cuts what looks like a command line of the platform or a path from the text, the footer lines and the file, adds one sentence ("The rest is done on the platform's page.") and records an event (`messaging_guard.EVENTS`; the log says the kinds, never the text). The fields of an operation's result that hold a terminal command or a path (`command`, `commands`, `path`, `dir`, `folder`, a held task's `next`) are left out of a typed command's reply on a phone by name (`ops_say._phone_view`).

An effect card draws no action the policy refuses, and offers none as a typed line: it says "Act on it on the platform's page." (with a link when `page_url` is set). The weekday abbreviation the operations layer writes before a date (`Wed 21/10/2026 13:00-14:00`) is said in the project's language on a phone (`phrases.localize_dates`: `qua 21/10/2026 13:00–14:00`), at the send point only: the stored card, its hash and the approval are of the stored text. A card's header names the kind as a plain word (`pedido #3`, `request #3`: `KIND_EFFECT`). The line that says a card waits for approval (`ACCT_CARD_SENT`) is not sent when the card itself was just sent to the same chat, and stays where none was shown. When an approved effect has run, the chat is told what was done in plain words (`runtime/messaging_outcome.py`, keys `OUT_`: the title and the moment of an event only when the card showed them, counts for the mailbox, never an address or a file name) and, when it was not done, why and what to check (`runtime/messaging_failure.py`, keys `FAIL_`: a folder that could not be read, an account not authorized, no room, a name taken, too large, no answer; the providers' own words are matched best effort and the rest is "It was not done"); the full English text stays on the terminal and the page. No effect is queued between the approval and the write today, so no "I will tell you when" is promised.

**A question the router left open** (package LIVE-2, reworked by slice S4 and the maintainer's decision 229 (1) of 2026-10-20). On a messaging app the question is BOUND to the sender it was asked of (`runtime/pending_items.py`, kind `router`: memory only, one binding for a project, a channel and a sender, replaced by a new request made from that key, cleared by a reply that names no route): only that sender's plain line, on that channel, within `pending_items.ROUTER_WINDOW` (one hour, the maintainer's), may answer it. Another sender, another channel, a line after the hour and a restart (the binding is lost) find none, and the line is a new request. Nothing is cancelled by code when the hour runs out: the old request stays `requested` and its question stays open (the page still shows it). Within the hour, a line that code recognises as a request of its own is a new line for every sender, the interpreter's included (code decides it; the model is not called). For a sender whom the interpreter reads, the question is, for the lines no list of code claims, one item of the typed frame (`i1`: its kind `router-question`, its need `text` and its age, and never a word of the question, nor its number) and the interpreter tells an answer from a new line: `answer` of `i1` is the answer, `route` is a new request, an `answers_item` that is not `i1` is the fixed ask-again sentence and the question stays open (`check` refuses it, `E_ITEM_UNKNOWN`), an operation of the catalog is carried out and the question stays open. With no decision (the service failed, a limit), and for a sender the interpreter does not read, the earlier rule holds: a line that code recognises as a request of its own (`ops_natural.recognises`: any tier-1 form, a list or any other read included; a write to the person's accounts; a reminder; the answer to the sender's own draft) is a new line, never the answer, before any frame, and is served while the question stays open, unchanged and unmentioned (slice S4b: no sentence about it is added to the reply); a typed command and a word that decides the card that is the last message are read before any question; only a question about the state ("progress"), a plain sentence and a reply (the reply gesture) to the question's own message may be the answer. A line taken as the answer is told so (`ANSWER_TAKEN`), and the footer on a phone is a plain phrase (`ASK_NEXT_PHONE`), not the `/new` line. The page and the terminal keep the conversation's question - the router's open question on its last request - until it is answered or cancelled. A reply that code gave to a line whose request it cancelled by code (`code:unclassified-phone`, `code:direct`, `code:operation`) is stored under that request, so that the next line is never taken for the answer to an older request's question (an `ask` reply too: it is shown by its run, limit L50). Such a request is marked (`conversation:code-closed:<id>`), and the router's memory neither reads its reply as a settled exchange nor includes it (`chat_memory`, `quiet`). Where code cannot enforce it: a free-text line meant as new work can still answer a live question inside the hour, and a request in words that no list of code claims (a calendar read in a language with no table, or in words no tier-1 form carries; "mostra minha agenda" is one since S4b) is told from the answer by the interpreter's one call alone, so a sender the interpreter does not read, or a call that failed, finds it taken as the answer; the hour, the binding and `ANSWER_TAKEN` are the protection.

**The class and the interface.** A messenger is a provider of the class `messenger:<platform>` (`providers/messenger/<impl>.py`, `providers/messenger/README.md`; the variables `MESSENGER_<PLATFORM>_PROVIDER`, then `MESSENGER_PROVIDER`): a script with four subcommands, JSON on stdout. `capabilities` says what the platform can draw (`max_text`, `buttons`, `max_buttons`, `max_button_label`, `lists`, `max_list_rows`, `url_buttons`, `reply_context`, `documents` and, optional, `listen` and `actions_need_reply`: on a platform that declares the second, the bridge acts on a typed approve, reject, release or answer only in reply to the card of that decision, L45); `receive --offset --timeout` returns the updates since an offset (`update_id`, `sender`, `chat`, `private`, `text`, `action`, `action_ref`, `reply_to`); `send --chat --message-file` sends the neutral message `{"text", "actions": [{"id", "label"}], "link", "document"}` and returns the platform's message id; `ack --action-ref` acknowledges a tapped button. A provider refuses a message beyond its capabilities; the bridge never sends one, it folds what does not fit into typed lines (`Send /approve 12`), which always work. Adding a platform adds a file, a registry row for its credential and an entry under `messaging.senders` in a project's configuration; nothing in the bridge changes. Telegram (`providers/messenger/telegram.py`) uses the Bot API with long polling (never a webhook), the text rendered from Markdown to Telegram's HTML parse mode (everything escaped first, links shown as `text (url)`, link previews off, a plain resend when Telegram refuses the formatting; `providers/messenger/README.md`), and the secret `MESSENGER_TELEGRAM_BOT_TOKEN` (`contracts/secrets.md`), which is in the request path and therefore redacted from every error path.

**Notices and the ledger.** Every `--notice-every` seconds, for each project whose policy permits reads, each open decision that has no card with the same content hash in a listed sender's chat gets one: a header `<project name> · <kind> #<id>`, the body, the actions. One decision per message; at most 10 cards per project and tick, then one line "And <n> more waiting. Send /pending to list them." With `messaging.notices: "text"` (default) the body is the decision's own text, cut with "(continues on the page)" or, when the platform takes documents, sent whole as a document; with `"count"` it is "A <kind> waits for you." and nothing of the project's text. A resolved decision's card is not edited in this version; acting on it later gets the operation's refusal as the reply. The ledger, `<state folder>/messaging-<platform>.json` (one per user and platform), is written through a temporary file of mode 0600 (`O_EXCL`), published atomically, and holds no text of a project: for each card the platform, the project id, the pending id and kind, the content hash (a plan's hash, an effect's payload hash, else the sha256 of the decision's text), the chat, the platform's message id, the action ids made for it and the time; the next update offset; and `seq`, the number action ids are made from. A card is found by platform, project id and chat, so two sets of projects on one machine never suppress each other's cards. A restart sends no card twice. A line kept for a busy project is not in the ledger: it is in the bridge's memory only ("Waiting lines" below). **Migration, once:** the ledger of the first versions lived in `<data_dir of the first project>/messaging-<platform>.json`; when the new file does not exist and that one does, the cards of the projects this bridge serves are copied into the new file with the old `seq` and `offset` (an action id on a card already sent keeps acting on that card), the old file is left in place, and one line says it can be deleted. Only the first served project's earlier ledger file is read, so a project whose earlier cards sat in another project's data folder may get its open decisions carded once more.

**The listen mode** (MSG-5). A provider that declares `"listen": true` in its capabilities (absent means false; Telegram leaves it out, Discord, WhatsApp, the official WhatsApp Cloud API and WhatsApp through Twilio declare it and work only in this mode) is not called once per verb: the bridge starts its long-lived verb `listen --platform <p>` once, through `resolve.start`, and keeps it. Standard input and output carry JSON lines (UTF-8, ASCII-escaped, one flush per line, at most 1 MiB each): the bridge writes `{"id", "op": "send", "chat", "message"}`, the provider writes `{"event": "ready"}`, `{"event": "update", "update": {...}}` (the shape of one element of `receive`), `{"event": "error", "kind", "reason"}` and `{"id", "ok", ...}` answers. There is no `ack`: the provider acknowledges its own taps at once and emits `action_ref: null` (the bridge also forces it to null), and no offset applies (the ledger's offset is not touched). The bridge side is `ListenSession` in `runtime/messaging_provider.py`: a writer thread owns stdin, a reader thread validates every line and every update (a line over the limit, not JSON, an unknown event, an answer to an unknown id, an update that is not private or lacks a sender or a chat: logged by class and dropped) and feeds a bounded update queue (the newest are dropped when it is full), a thread drains stderr keeping the last 300 characters (never logged), and a watcher on `proc.wait()` detects the exit, kills the leader's group and fails every waiting request at once. Notices wait until `ready` (no card goes to a provider that is not ready) and are looked at again the moment it is. A provider that does not say `ready` in 180 s counts as a crash. An exit 2 or 3 stops the bridge (exit 3, with the reason of the provider's last error event, else its stderr tail, else `exit <n>`), with one exception: an exit 3 right after an error event of kind `paused` (the platform session is gone and only the person can mend it, as a logged-out WhatsApp device) does not stop the bridge. The supervisor logs the reason, waits 5 minutes (a close interrupts the wait) and starts the provider again; that start is not charged to the ceiling below and the backoff is left as it is; the line is written again every 24 hours while the provider stays paused (never the oftener, never an id), and again after the provider has been connected in between; cards wait as they do for any provider that is not ready, and what was sent to the platform meanwhile is delivered by the platform when the provider reconnects (at least once, as on Telegram), each approval still bound to the card the person saw. Any other exit starts it again after 1, 2, 4 ... up to 300 s (the first wait again only after it stayed ready for 10 minutes), and at most 200 starts per rolling 24 hours for each bridge process, after which the bridge stops with a sentence (a platform may reset a credential that connects too often). **A send can be answered `deferred`** (MSG-8): `{"id", "ok": false, "kind": "deferred", "reason"}` is a platform that lets a business write first only inside a window (the official WhatsApp API: 24 hours after the person's last message) saying it cannot take the card now. `deferred` is an answer kind only (`ANSWER_KINDS` in `runtime/messaging_provider.py`), never an error-event kind and never a reason to stop or restart. The bridge records nothing in the ledger for that card, so it is offered again at the next notice period and at once when the person next writes from that chat (`Bridge.deferred_chats`), and it logs one line for a card and a chat at most every 24 hours (never every tick, never an id or text). A provider that answers it has done what it can for the person meanwhile (the WhatsApp one sends a fixed template, at most once for each closed window and again at most every 7 days). **Delivery is at least once:** a send in flight when the provider dies fails and its card is sent again after the next `ready`; the first copy, if it was delivered, has buttons the ledger has no record of, which answer the stale-button sentence. On the bridge's stop the session closes after the jobs have ended (their replies still need the provider): stdin is closed, then after 10 s SIGTERM goes to the provider's group, then after 5 s SIGKILL, by group id only. A listen provider has a session of its own, so it must exit within 5 s of the end of its stdin: that is the only thing that stops it when the bridge is killed. Tests: `runtime/tests/test_messaging_listen.py` over `runtime/tests/fixtures/messenger_listen_standin.py`.

**Telegram's two 409 answers.** A `getUpdates` 409 whose description says another `getUpdates` request terminated this one means another program is reading the bot's updates (a second bridge, or another tool with the same token): the provider says so and exits 3, like the 409 that names a webhook (which keeps its sentence about `deleteWebhook`); a provider has no exit 4 (`providers/CONTRACT.md`), and the bridge's fatal path prints the sentence and exits 3. The bridge's own lock refusal is the 4 of `messaging.py`, which is not a provider.

**Inbound.** An update from a sender who is not in `messaging.senders.<platform>` of an accepted configuration, or from a chat that is not private, is dropped with no reply (the log says "dropped an update from an unlisted sender" and nothing else). A tapped button is looked up in the ledger (an unknown one is answered "This button is no longer valid. Send /pending."), acknowledged, and run with the recorded pending id and, for a plan, the recorded hash; a plan that changed since the card is refused by `ops.approve` and a new card is sent. A reply to a question's card is `answer`; any other text is `say`, in a thread, one model call per project at a time, and its reply is sent back in the same neutral message (Markdown, which the provider renders or sends plain) split to the platform's limit. A line that meets a busy project waits ("Waiting lines" below); a command and a tap never wait ("Still working on the last message.").

**Waiting lines** (BQ-1). A text line that would run `say` (a plain line, `/new`, `/ask`; `messaging_bridge.queueable`) and meets a busy project is kept by the bridge instead of refused: when a model job of the project holds its slot (`shell_kit.Busy`), or `say` answers `RunBusy` (the router answers another request, for instance one of the local service). The bridge keeps at most 1 line per chat and 5 per project, and answers "Got it: I will answer this after the current one."; beyond those caps, and for a command (`/progress`, `/cancel`, `/approve`...) or a tap, the sentence of before stands ("Still working on the last message." for a held slot, "The planning agent is answering another request. Send it again in a moment." for `RunBusy`). The oldest line of a project whose slot is free is started at each iteration of the loops and at the end of each job, through `Bridge.run_op`, so the sender's policy is read again when the line runs (limit L46; a typed `/ask` needs the group `ask`, not `talk`, when it comes and again when it runs: `Bridge.needs`): a sender removed from the configuration meanwhile is dropped with no reply (an unlisted sender never gets one), a sender narrowed is refused with the policy's sentence, and a sender whose profile does not let them talk is refused at once and nothing is kept. A line that met `RunBusy` waits one more second before it is tried again, keeping its place (about 1 s on a listen provider, within 2 s on a polling one; a held slot is looked at when its job ends, through the kit's `job_ended` hook). A listen provider whose session is not ready starts no waiting line (expiry still applies). A project whose configuration is no longer accepted drops the line like an unlisted sender. The lines start in the order they were kept: a line that comes while the oldest is being started goes behind it. A line waiting 1800 seconds is dropped with "Not answered: the project stayed busy for 30 minutes. Send it again."; on SIGINT or SIGTERM each chat with a waiting line gets "Not answered: the bridge stopped. Send it again." (best effort, before the runs end, all together and within 10 seconds in all, so that a provider that hangs cannot hold back the end of the runs). The lines are in the bridge's memory only, because the ledger "holds no text of a project": the text is never logged, never written to the ledger or to a file, and a crash loses the lines (the person sends them again). The order across shells is not first in, first out: the page's queue (above) and this one are separate, and a line of the page can run before a waiting line of a phone. The queue is not the store's (`ops_say.QUEUING_CHANNELS` stays `("page",)`): that one is drained only by the local service, and its entry does not know the chat to answer.

**Configuration.** `docs/workbench/runtime.json` may hold `"messaging": {"senders": {"<platform>": ["<sender id>"]}, "notices": "text" | "count", "page_url": null | "http(s)://..."}` and `"channels"` (above). Nothing in them is a secret (the bot's token is read by the provider from the secret store). As every change of the file, they take effect only after `accept-config` is typed in the terminal; the bridge reads the accepted settings at each call, so a change the person accepted takes effect without a restart, and a configuration that is not accepted refuses everything.

**Privacy.** What the person writes, and every notice in `text` mode, passes the platform's servers: a bot chat on Telegram, and a direct message on Discord, is not end-to-end encrypted. On the official WhatsApp Cloud API (`whatsapp-cloud`) Meta processes the messages as the API's host and the webhook reaches the machine through a public HTTPS address the person chooses (any tunnel or proxy; who can read the traffic in transit depends on it, `providers/messenger/README.md`, "A public HTTPS address for the official WhatsApp providers"). Through Twilio (`whatsapp-twilio`) Twilio and Meta both process them, and a message sent while the machine is asleep is lost, because Twilio retries a webhook once and only on a connection failure ("WhatsApp through Twilio"). WhatsApp's messages on the unofficial client are end-to-end encrypted to the linked device, which decrypts them on the machine that runs the bridge; WhatsApp sees who writes to whom and when. `count` keeps the notices free of the project's text, while the replies to what the person writes still carry text. A person who wants more safety leaves the policy closed and uses the page. In `count` mode an effect's card still carries the approve command, which holds the project's folder path and the effect's hash. `ops.config` (the page's config route) now returns the sender ids and the channel policy; neither is a secret. The bridge reads `pending` itself, outside the policy's `read` group, to build cards and bind approvals. A typed `/approve` is never passed to `say` from `messaging`: only the bridge's own `/approve <id>` (bound to the card) and the buttons approve.

**Files from messaging apps** (ATT-1b). A provider that declares `attachments` puts the files of a message in the update (`attachments`, at most 10) and serves `fetch-attachment` (or the listen op `fetch`); the bridge turns the update into the intent `attachment`, and `/add <n>` and `/discard <n>` into `add` and `discard`. `runtime/messaging_attach.py` does the rest (limits L47 and L48, "The sources" above): the policy of the sender first, then the checks on what the platform declared, then the fetch in a job thread of the bridge, `attachment-hold` through `Bridge.run_op`, and a card with **Add** and **Discard** bound in the ledger (kind `attachment`, `attachment_id`) to the hash of the file, its target and its label. A tap or `/add` re-reads the held list, compares the hash, asks the policy for the sender who acts and runs `attachment-add` in a job; `/pending` also lists the files that wait. With the grant `add-sources-direct` there is no card: the hold adds at once. The caption is never read but as an exact `@<source id>`.

**Reminders** (RM-1). A reminder is a stored one-shot notice: the person's own words, sent once at a moment to the chat that made it, by the bridge, with no model between. `/remind <time> <text>`, `/reminders` and `/forget <n>` (the intents `remind`, `reminders` and `forget` of `runtime/messaging_inbound.py`) go through `Bridge.run_op` as the operations `remind`, `reminders` and `reminder-forget` (and `reminder-restore`, NR-1), which are the group `reminders` (in no default policy: a project turns it on in a profile, and `accept-config --widen` confirms it; never on the MCP mode, no row lists it). The time is `HH:MM` (today, or tomorrow when it has passed), `YYYY-MM-DD HH:MM` or `in <n>m|h|d`, read against the machine's clock and zone with a 24-hour clock of two digits; anything that can be read two ways is refused with the usage sentence, a wall time that a clock change skips is refused, and a reminder is at least one minute and at most 366 days ahead. The text is one line of at most 300 characters (a format character such as a bidirectional override, a zero-width character or the byte order mark is dropped) and is refused if it looks like a credential. At most 100 wait in a project, 30 for one chat (so one sender cannot block the others), and 30 are made for one chat in a day (the local day). From the terminal or the page `--to <platform>:<sender id>` names a listed sender who holds the group. At the end of each tick of the bridge `reminders_due` claims what is due (`sending`), and the bridge sends it to that chat and marks it `sent`; a send that failed or was deferred is open again and tried after a pause of 1, 2, 4, 8 ... minutes (at most 64), until the 6-hour window ends it; a claim older than 10 minutes belongs to a bridge that stopped and is open again (a line may go twice, never zero times); one more than 6 hours past its time ends `missed` and one late by less goes out marked "(due at HH:MM)" when it is more than 5 minutes late; a sender removed from the list, or whose profile no longer holds the group, when the reminder fires gets nothing and it ends `dropped`. With `notices: count` the phone gets "A reminder is due (#n)." and no text, and `/reminders` lists numbers and times. The words are in the store (`reminders`, migration 20) and in no other place (limit L51); a phone is never told a path.

**Reminders in the person's own words** (NR-1). A line that holds a remind word ("me lembra amanha as 13h da aula de violao", "remind me tomorrow at 1 pm to call the florist", "em 25 min me avisa de ..." with the word anywhere in the line), a list ("mostra os lembretes que estao abertos", "what reminders do I have"), a forget ("esquece o lembrete #3", "forget reminder 3", "apaga o lembrete da manicure") or an undo ("desfaz", "undo") is matched by code (`natural.candidate`, from `messaging_inbound.parse`, the intent `natural`) to the operation the typed command runs, and `/remind <rest>` that the strict grammar above does not claim ("/remind aula de violao as 13hrs amanha") is read the same way. A reply gesture to a CARD answers that card, whatever the text looks like; a reply to any other message is read as the line it is, and a reply to the draft's own question answers the draft. No model runs, no route lock is taken and no model slot is held, so a reminder is not held behind a running router. The time is `natural_time.parse` of the person's line with the clock, translated into the three forms above, so `reminders.resolve` keeps every check it has; the words of the reminder are the line minus the trigger words, the time phrases and the connectives at its edges ("de", "to", never a number), cleaned and scanned for credentials as before. `remind` takes the person's line as `said` (and `how`: `trigger`, `bare`, `note` or `command`; and `draft`, the question it answers) after `ops_actor.guard`, so a profile without the group gets the typed refusal before the line is read (a line that only looks like a request, such as a bare "undo", is a plain line for such a sender). Three tiers decide the rest of the shape: this is tier 1 (code, 0 model runs); the router's run is tier 3 and embeddings tier 2, both later packages.

The rule that governs it is the maintainer's: anything ambiguous, or not fully understood, is ASKED BACK and never guessed; and a reminder asked in FREE TEXT (natural words, `/remind` with natural arguments) is NEVER made at once: the reading is SHOWN ("Entendi: amanha, seg 19/10, as 09:00 (BRT): ligar pro fisioterapeuta. Confirma?") and the reminder is made only after the person's "sim" - a one-tap Yes button where the platform has buttons (`Bridge.ask_yes`, bound in the ledger as a card of the kind `reminder` that holds no word of the reminder; a tap on the button of a question that is no longer the open one is told so and makes nothing), and the typed "yes" everywhere. Only the STRICT `/remind` forms (`HH:MM text`, `YYYY-MM-DD HH:MM text`, `in Nm|h|d text`) make a reminder directly. `natural.understand` never returns a reminder to make; `natural.complete` returns one only for a confirmed draft (a yes to the confirmation that showed the whole reading, or the answer to the kind question, which shows it too; a pick among the readings offered, a time, a day, "another time" or the words are never a yes: they lead to the question that shows the reading they produced, with the Yes button), what is made is the moment that was SHOWN (`natural._pinned`: its day and hour, never a bare `HH:MM` or an `in 30m` that would be read again at the time of the answer; an absolute moment that has passed by the answer is refused as past and never moved to another day, and a relative one is shown again with its new moment and asked again), and `remind(said=...)` checks the moment and the words (a moment that has passed, a date the calendar lacks, a credential) BEFORE the reading is shown, so a refusal never has a question. That makes a silently wrong reminder impossible by construction, and what remains is the cost of a wrong reading shown, which the corpus test counts as a metric. The reply to the yes shows the reading and the undo, in the project's language, with the relative word first: "Lembrete #4: amanha, seg 19/10, as 13:00 (BRT): aula de violao. Para desfazer: /forget 4." (the words only when the notices are `text`; a year that is not this one is shown; a time of day that had passed says so in the question, "13:00 de hoje ja passou: amanha, ...", and gives tomorrow's; a date that is today with an hour that has passed is refused as past, never moved to next year). The same reply answers the typed `/remind`; the terminal and the page keep the sentence they always had (`reply` of the operation), and `/reminders` shows each moment as a person reads it. What is unclear is asked back, with a draft kept in the bridge's memory for that chat (ten minutes, swept on every tick, one per chat, lost on a restart, never logged, never in the store): an hour with no `h`, no `:` and no marker ("as 9", "as 3 e meia") asks 09:00 or 21:00 ("9h", "13h30", "13.30" with a cue or a meridiem and "1h30" after "daqui a" are not asked; "1h30" alone is a clock or a duration); "sexta que vem" or "next friday" offers the nearest one and the one after it; "sexta a meia-noite" offers the start and the end of the day; a day with no hour, a vague time, two time phrases or a date such as 5/6 in an English line asks which; x/y is a date only after a cue ("dia", "on") or beside a time, and never a fraction ("parcela 3/10", "3/10 do aluguel"); a remind word with no time asks when; a note word with no time ("anota: ...") offers a reminder; a time and no words asks what (a short answer that is not a request for work, thanks, yes or no, and a bare time completes the time, not the text). The reading is CONFIRMED ("Entendi: amanha, seg 19/10, as 09:00: arrumar o quarto. Confirma? (sim / outra hora)") when a word of a week, a month or a year stands within two words of the time ("semana que vem", "next month"), a day word has a typo (read as the word it is one letter from), a dotted number stands beside a day word with no "as" ("amanha 13.30"), a past date this year is read as next year's, or an abbreviation of a day that may be a name does not touch the time or a number ("sex shop as 9h": read as the day and confirmed, never dropped; "Fri at 9am", "Seg as 9h" and "May 5" touch the time or a number and are the day); "sim" makes it (courtesies around it are ignored: "sim, pode", "yes please"), an hour or a day answers only the part it names, a day and an hour replace both (and the new reading is shown before it is made), "outra hora" asks "Para quando?", keeps the words and the day that was shown (an hour or a bare hour answered is on that day), a "sim mas as 15h" is a yes with a new hour, and "nao", "nao obrigado" or "cancela" gives it up. A line that repeats is said to be a routine's, since a reminder happens once, and nothing is made or kept: the repeating word counts only when it is part of the time phrase ("toda segunda", "a cada 2 horas", "de 8 em 8 horas", "every monday", "todo dia as 9h", "daily at 9"); the same words inside the reminder's own text ("weekly report", "each supplier", "every client", "a cada um", "todo dia da planilha") are text. A line that names a week and no day ("semana que vem as 10h", "next week at 10") asks "Qual dia da semana que vem?" (no tomorrow is offered) and the weekday answered is the one of that week; a part of the day beside an hour with no marker says which hour ("amanha de manha as 9" is 09:00, "hoje a noite as 9" and "tonight at 9" are 21:00). A line with a time and neither a remind word nor a calendar word ("aula de violao amanha 13h", "anote ...") asks reminder or event; a scheduling verb with a time and no calendar word ("agende ...", "schedule ...") asks "event, or something else?", where "something else" sends the whole line on to the planning agent and "reminder" keeps it; "marca ... segunda 13h" and a verb with a calendar word ("marca na agenda ...") are event writes, which the calendar (not built yet) answers in the project's language, after the policy of a plain line, and makes no reminder; a bare calendar noun ("agenda de amanha?", "calendar for tomorrow?") is a read and goes on to the planning agent. The next line of that chat finishes the draft when it is only a time, one of the readings offered (by the day and the hour together), a day and an hour that replace them, the kind, or the missing words; a bare hour answers "a que horas?" (and is asked 09:00 or 21:00), "22/10" answers a confirmation with a day, "13.30" answers "Quando?" with an hour, and "sim" to "a reminder or an event" is the reminder. A short reply that does not fit the question ("sim" to two readings, "hmm") is asked once more; a longer line, a request for work, a question, another request of this tier, a command, a file or a reply to another message drops the draft and is handled as a new line, and a new question that replaces an open one begins "A pergunta anterior foi descartada.". A "sim" that comes after the ten minutes are over is told that the question is no longer open (only the fact that a question expired is remembered, for thirty minutes, never what it said). "Marca um lembrete ...", "agende um lembrete ..." and "schedule a reminder ..." make a reminder: the word reminder wins over the scheduling verb.

A forget acts at once only on an explicit number ("#9", "lembrete 9", "lembrete numero 9", "lembrete n. 9", "n 9", "no. 9", "no9", "nr 9"); with no noun ("cancela #3", "cancela o 3") it is shown and confirmed, and for a sender who has no such reminder it is a plain line; a forget by an hour in front of the noun ("cancel the 9am reminder") is matched against the moment and confirmed, and one whose words are only numbers is shown, never acted on; a number after any other word ("lembrete do 3", "lembrete de 2") or several numbers ("lembrete 1 e 2") are shown with what they name and cancelled only on a "sim" ("Cancelar o lembrete #3: ...? (sim / nao)"; a "nao" says that nothing was cancelled, and "sim, cancela" is a yes); an hour ("o lembrete das 9") is matched against the moment of the sender's own waiting reminders and confirmed the same way; a number inside other words ("2 boletos", "do dia 3") is a word, looked for by whole words among that sender's own waiting reminders (one match cancels it, several send the list and ask, none says so); a word right after "lembrete" with no connective ("reminder banner from the site") is not about a reminder of the person's and goes on. Every forget reply shows what was cancelled (its moment and, in text mode, its words) and "desfaz": the operation `reminder-restore` (the group `reminders`, the same channels as `remind`) brings back the most recent reminder the sender cancelled in the last ten minutes that is not already back (each has its own ten minutes; the bare word only: "desfaz", "undo", optionally with "o lembrete", never "undo the deploy"), as a NEW reminder with the same words, moment, zone and chat (the record of the cancelled one is never changed), not when its time is less than a minute away and not when it is already back ("O lembrete #9 esta de volta como #12"); the terminal and the page may name one with `--id`.

**The guard** (NR-1). Before a reading is made, the words of the line OUTSIDE the time phrases are read once more (`natural_time.leftover_doubt`), and the reading is shown and confirmed, never made, when they hold: a month, a weekday, a day word or an ordinal date of any table (an abbreviation such as "sex" or "out" counts where its language is the line's, so "take the bread out" is English), a date marker with a number beside it ("dia 5", "on 5"), the name of a day of the year from a table's list ("Natal", "Christmas", "Easter"), or, for a line that has no trigger word of a table, is not a typed command and names no day or length of time, a word that no table holds (the line may be in a language there is no table for: "manana a las 15:00", "vendredi a 21h30"; the moment the engine read is what is shown, and the person says the day). A weak word of a day that is not where a day stands (it touches neither the time nor a number) is no date: it stays in the reminder's words and the guard confirms the reading. A month with a day number beside it and no cue ("May 5", "5 May", "the 5th of May") is read as the date and shown with its year. The ordinal of the first ("1o", "primeiro de dezembro", "dia primeiro"), "quarter past", "quarter to", "half past" and an hour said in words ("as nove") are table data. A part of the day that has passed ("this morning at 9" said in the afternoon, "tonight at 9" at 22:30) is shown as tomorrow's, the same weekday as today ("sunday" on a Sunday) as today or the next one, and a length of days or weeks ("in 3 days") asks the hour. Whatever a person answers to "which day next week?" is shown with the day it resolved to (the week after this one, Monday to Sunday) and made only on a yes, and so is a line that names a weekday of next week ("tuesday next week", "semana que vem segunda"). The rule is tested on two corpora (`runtime/tests/test_natural_corpus.py`): 575 labelled lines of the date-parser spike (Portuguese, English, Spanish, French, German, Japanese) and the probe lines of the independent reviews. The test fails on a line made at a moment other than the expected one, a line that had to be asked and was made, and a line that is not a time line and was made; a question or a confirmation never fails it.

The words are data, not code: a language is one file `runtime/natural_lang_<tag>.py` holding a `TABLE` literal (the schema is in `runtime/natural_time.py`) and one name in `runtime/natural_languages.json`, which lists the tables shipped (an empty list stops the bridge). The files are read as data (`ast.literal_eval`, never executed), validated once when a bridge starts (a table that breaks the schema, or two with the same tag, stops it with a sentence), and the table of every language is tried on every line (a Portuguese project understands an English line); a word that is also a common word of another language (an abbreviation such as "sex" or "may") counts only in a line that is in its language, stays in the words and is confirmed where it may be a name, and "may" with no day number beside it is just a word; the common misspellings of "tomorrow" and "hoje" ("tmrw", "tommorow", "hje") are entries of the table. A line no table parses is not matched (ask-back now, the router later). v1 ships `en` and `pt-BR`; adding one is a table, a name in the list and its tests, with no edit of the engine.

## What the proof covers

The battery measured one skill, in one run, with an input in the form of its cases. That holds for the runtime because the container and the entry are the same. What the runtime adds was not measured: resuming a task with the person's answers in the request, and running a skill on a document another model wrote. For those the only check is field evidence, recorded from the first task. The runtime uses a band only after checking that the measurement files of its checkout are the recorded ones and that the image is the evidence's; when either fails, the pair is unproven: the run is supervised whatever the mode, on the model the person chose or the one `auto` falls back to. A skill runs in the condition it was measured in.
